<span id="page-146" class="sec-page-anchor" aria-hidden="true"></span><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Consultation held aboard the</span> <em>MARY</em> <span class="sc">by President Fremlen and Council and William Bayley, Thomas Steevens, and Thomas Tomplins (Purser), January 3, 1639</span> (<em>Marine Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. iv. no. 16).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">James Bycraft, boatswain, is charged with mutinous behaviour and is sentenced to lose his wages from the date of his offence, to be ducked three times from the yardarm, and to be kept in irons during the homeward voyage. Meredith Jones and Robert Jelly, his associates, are pardoned on promise not to repeat the offence. <em>PS.</em>—The ship being about to depart and Bycraft being brought out to be ducked, the President and Council are pleased to remit this part of his punishment, at the request of Bayley and Methwold and 'for a good oman to the voyage that it may begin rather with mercie then inflictinge of castigacion'. They also allow him his wages till the date of his being put in irons. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">President Fremlen and Council</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-001" href="#sec-06-fn-001" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-001" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, <span class="sc">aboard the</span> <em>MARY</em> <span class="sc">at the mouth of the Surat River, to the Company, January 4, 1639</span> (<em>O.C.</em> 1656).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Enclose a transcript of their letter of February 9, 1638 [<em>missing</em>], sent by the <em>Jonas</em>, which sailed the following day; also copies of correspondence with the other factories. Received the Company's advices of March 16 last by the <em>Discovery</em>, which arrived September 23. Narrative of her voyage. The complaints about the silk concern the Persia factors, to whom the Company's letters have been communicated by the <em>Diamond</em>, which sailed on November 7. Express regret that they failed to advise that the China stuffs were defective, but refer to the letters, &amp;c. from Goa for proof that they were not guilty of dishonesty in the matter. Some of the China twisted silk missing at the time was found and sent home in the <em>William</em>. They do their best as regards the quality and price of calicoes, but cannot help fluctuations in cost or some variations in quality, due to the factors at other stations <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-147" data-page="147" data-folio="93" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n146_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 93">[p. 93]</button> accepting cloth inferior to the approved samples. In future will disregard small differences in price when making up the parcels. The directions about calicoes have been communicated to the factories concerned. As to Agra cloth, ‘it is litle worne in these partes, although for divers yeares past great quantityes have bine brought hither and exported. The Deriabauds and Keriabauds come allwaies cured from thence, but not soe well as if they were done in Guzeratt; but they are brought soe to Agra and cannot bee had browne. The Mercoolyes on the other side they are browne and cannot bee whited theare as they should bee.’ A parcel of these was bought at Agra last summer, but first the rains and then the want of transport (owing to the King’s progress to Lahore) prevented its dispatch until October 4, and so it will come too late for England this season. Send some ‘Keriabauds’ which failed to sell at Mokha. These are bought and sold at Agra so many to a ‘rupia’. Explain their reasons for employing Druce at Baroda; Samuel Pauncefote is now in charge there, and they trust that his care in the choice of cloth will give satisfaction. Rejoice to find the ‘Sinda’ goods approved. ‘Wee should have continued a factory theare, if Cobbes depradations had not disgraced us from thence. When that businesse was overblowne, wee resetled againe.’ On Fremlen’s diversion to Gombroon, John Spiller and John Stanford were moved from Broach to ‘Sinda’, whence they are expected shortly to return with a good cargo. Cotton yarn to be sent in the <em>Mary</em> and the <em>Swan</em>. Have often asked their friends at Bantam to supply them annually with pepper, but hitherto their requests have been ignored, and so they are forced to buy pepper in the Deccan at high rates. ‘The coast of Mallabarre undoubtedly produceth great quantityes, but the principall growth is about Onor [Honawār], Barcelor, and Battecale neare Goa, and Cochine and Coulaon [Quilon] further to the southwards; all which places are subject to the Portugalls or obliged by contract to deliver theire pepper at a certaine price; and if the <em>Planter</em> procured a small quantity, it was by way of insinuation, as praetending peace with the Portugalls; for the Hollanders, that have allready two yeares together blockt upp Goa and domineered alongst the whole coast, could procure no pepper from thence, insomuch that they also buy in Decan as wee have done this last yeare.’ It sells to good profit <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-148" data-page="148" data-folio="94" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n147_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 94">[p. 94]</button> in Persia and India, being worth at Ahmadābād nearly 30 mahmūdīs the maund. So long as the Surat junks traded freely with Achin and brought thence quantities of pepper, it was sold here at 14–16 mahmūdīs the maund; but now that the trade with that place has failed, the supply depends entirely upon the Deccan, and the cost of land transport has raised the price here to 25 mahmūdīs. Vīrjī Vōra consequently finds himself unable to fulfil his contract with them; ‘that, with some other praetences as unreasonable, having cooperated with the late Governor of Suratt his most barbarous tyranny to accumulate troubles uppon that miserable man, intended to his subversion if the Kings justice releive him not, now when with much difficulty it is arrived unto his knowledge.’ As regards the <em>Planter’s</em> cargo, they hear that the Viceroy complains of the English having given double the contract price for pepper; the benzoin seems to be a larger quantity than the Company have imported from the beginning of their trade, and so it will not readily be got rid of; while the cinnamon is probably of the poorer kind called ‘cinamon de mato<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-002" href="#sec-06-fn-002" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-002" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’. The remaining commodities are slight, ‘and soe is theire new factory’. Cannot understand the deficiency in the weight of the pepper sent home [in the <em>William</em>], unless it be that their computation of 27 lb. for each Deccan maund is too high. Saltpetre is certainly ‘a badd neighbour to better goods’, and since receiving the Company’s letter they have refrained from adding to their stock; but the commanders of the ships affirm that if pepper be shot among the saltpetre ‘it praeventeth all praejudice’, and as they have had an offer of some saltpetre from ‘Sinda’ which is both good and cheap, they will probably send home a further quantity. As explained by Robinson [<em>see</em> p. 91], the indigo complained of was bought through a Banyan, because at that time the factors dare not be seen to be in possession of such a quantity, lest the merchants robbed by Ayres should seize it. The two bales of earth have been sent to Ahmadābād, where Robinson will endeavour to obtain satisfaction. Note the quantities of indigo required in future. The gum-lac which the Company describe is probably ‘that which is found at Pegu and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-149" data-page="149" data-folio="95" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n148_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 95">[p. 95]</button> Tannasery, which noe part of the woorld besides can aequall. William Methwould remembreth to have sent you such from Mesulapatam, when the way to make use of the tincture was hardly knowne in England. That which this country affoordeth is of a farre different sort, and yet it is used for dying all the woorld over.' Will acquaint the subordinate factories with the Company's remarks, for this commodity is seldom bought at Surat. It is impossible to prevent the pilfering of calicoes, as the packers, carters, boatmen, and even the English sailors and soldiers, will steal them as opportunity occurs. Cannot, however, understand the deficiency in the bales from 'Sinda', for those were all packed in the presence of the Shāhbandar and sealed with his seal. The missing sample bales were duly signed for by Bradshaw, the purser of the <em>Discovery</em>, who (they hear) on his arrival in England 'returned to his former debauchure'. He was one of 'eight or tenne attendants which Captayne Weddall tooke money of to conduct and praefferre in India'; and while serving here was both well-behaved and useful. Have advised by the <em>Jonas</em> the mission of Fremlen to Persia. The dispatch of the <em>Swan</em> to the Red Sea could not be avoided, for the Indians will not believe their story of French pirates; 'all wee can say can hardly sattisfy them that there is such a nation as Frenchmen; in reguard that none of that name have ever yet traded amongst them, they thinke (and some of them doe not sticke to say soe) that it is a devise of ours, or rather a tricke put uppon them, that wee may robbe with the more impunety.' Hence the offer of convoy to Mōkha, as 'if they refuse or miscarry wee are fairely acquitted'. In regard to trade there, the Arabs, having expelled the Turks, are very desirous of commerce with all nations, as is shown by their fair entertainment of the English and affirmed in letters from the 'Emaume' [Imām] of Mokha; while no place is better or more profitable for a ship to winter in. It is true that for two years their trade has not been successful; but in the first of these the vessel failed to reach her port, and in the second the markets were overcrowded. Still, even this was better than sending the vessels round Madagascar, or allowing them to winter at 'Bombaya'. Besides, had the <em>Swan</em> gone to Masulipatam she would not have found sufficient cargo to lade her for England. Do not deny that they have felt aggrieved at the way in which the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-150" data-page="150" data-folio="96" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n149_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 96">[p. 96]</button> Presidents of Bantam have preferred their local interests to the general welfare; on which account in former letters 'wee were bold to declare our opinions that your affaires (like to those of other nations which are farr greater) would bee best mannadged if they were submitted under one government, which you might place at your pleasure either at Bantam or heere'. Acknowledge Willoughby's ability and zeal; but he showed himself always averse to anything proposed from Surat, and was a rigid censurer of their proceedings. Repudiate, however, the idea that such differences are allowed to prejudice the Company's interests; and instance in proof their supplies to Masulipatam. Could not foresee the unhappy accident which led to their gold being placed under sequestration at Goa; expect a favourable termination to the suit very shortly. Will narrate Weddell's proceedings later. Cannot refuse to carry freight goods, as this course adds to the revenues of the port, and the Governor of Surat, 'who hath little lesse then regall power over your estates and our persons,' would trouble them by all means in his power; but they have announced that they will not be responsible for any goods not declared when put aboard, 'nor for such neither yf the owners doe not except against them before they are out of our possession.' It should not be forgotten that the Company has benefited by the freight money to the extent of many thousands of pounds in the past five years. As regards this practice being used as a cloak for private trade, they cannot deny that 'in some particulars you have mett with the corruption of the times'; but such transgressions are either so small or so well concealed that they cannot accuse any man of exorbitancy deserving severe censure. There are, however, some distinctions to be made as regards private trade. For a factor to use the Company's stock for his own profit, or to take for his own benefit the pick of the investment, is of course most dishonest. To spoil the markets by selling in competition with the Company, or to pester their ships with private goods without payment, is prejudicial, but less dishonest. The dispatch of commodities to England is an interference with the Company's monopoly, and should be sternly suppressed, as also any similar trade to the southwards; but they can see no harm in an Englishman's goods being sent in the Company's ships to Mokha or Gombroon (freight being duly paid), <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-151" data-page="151" data-folio="97" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n150_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 97">[p. 97]</button> both being ‘common marts of these Easterne partes’. However, so far from defending private trade, ‘wee are confident to affirme that wee have suppressed it.’ Deny any confederacy with the ships’ officers in this respect ; and affirm that all goods taken on board by their warrant pay freight duly, though this is not the case with the seamen’s goods. The difficulty is to detect the offenders, for even the most abject here refuses to give evidence. In England an informer may be found, because he can be protected ; but here he is deterred by the fact that he must perforce for the rest of the voyage be at the mercy of the principal delinquents. Of course the ships’ officers, were they faithful, could easily prevent the taking aboard of any goods not consigned by the factors ; but it is difficult to find any means of forcing them to do this. Did their best to get the former accounts perfected ; now send a journal completed to the time of the departure of the <em>Mary</em>, and a balance to the time of the arrival of the <em>Discovery</em>. Regret to learn the ungrateful behaviour of Simon de Tavor, the brother-in-law of Father Reimão. Correa has long since left the factory and relinquished his monthly allowance, but he remains in Surat and lends assistance when required ; he thanks the Company for the delivery of his letters. Advised in their last what had been recovered from the plundered caravan ; no more is to be expected, in spite of the King’s farmān to the Governor of Baroda, in whose jurisdiction the robbery occurred. ‘It is true that those Rashpootes live like outlawes. They are auncient inhabitants of the country, which are not easily reduced to give unto Caesar that which is Caesars ; and therfore, inhabiting inaccessible places, they buy theire liberty with a constant poverty, sometimes living at peace with theire neigbore Governors, uppon condicions agreed uppon, sometimes overmasterd and overawed by the Governors vigilancy and power ; as it was in the time of Mirza Darab [Mīrzā Dārāb], who was then newly remooved that Meere Shumus [Mīr Shams] might succeede ; at which time your goods and servants most unhappily past that way and miscarried.’ No doubt such losses ought in fairness to be made good by the Mogul’s officers, ‘but equity in these parts is measured out unto us by theire will and conveniency,’ and it is considered to be sufficient answer that these Rājpūts are rebels. Wish they could say the same of their own countrymen, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-152" data-page="152" data-folio="98" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n151_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 98">[p. 98]</button> but it appears that those delinquents have the royal authority behind them—a fact which the Dutch have gladly communicated to the Governor and others, and which has tended to the discredit of His Majesty, who in his letters sent last year<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-003" href="#sec-06-fn-003" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-003" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> promised justice and satisfaction. As regards the computation of the capital in this country, allowance must be made for the money detained at Goa and for that remitted to Masulipatam; it will then be found that they depended wholly on the cargo of the <em>Jonas</em>, from which also they sent a stock to Bantam. Acknowledge the receipt of the cargo of the <em>Discovery</em>. The broadcloth, which is well-conditioned, has been taken by the Governor at 10 and 12½ rupees [per coved ?], a better price than formerly obtained. Part of the lead was sold to the Governor at 7½ mahmūdīs the maund; could not get more, because the Dutch sell at that price. The rest is reserved for sale to the Portuguese or at ‘Sinda’. Deficiency in the quicksilver, and the purser’s explanation. Knives, strong waters, paper, &amp;c., received. Defects in the surgeon’s chests. Trust that the rich cargoes returned in the <em>Jonas</em>, <em>Mary</em>, and <em>Swan</em> will encourage the adventurers to pursue the trade, which (if not interrupted by their own countrymen) would be as profitable as at any previous period. Cogan is well known to some of them as having served the Company from 1615; he has now been admitted to the Council, and his colleagues heartily wish there were more such men in India. On the Coromandel Coast there is not one able servant left, unless it be Francis Day. Cogan would make a very suitable Agent there; but the factors have been so lavish in expense that they have neither stock nor credit left, and ‘theare needes litle helpe to doe no businesse’. Besides, as Masulipatam is under Bantam, the factors may refuse to accept an Agent from Surat. However, they will risk the latter as soon as the former difficulty is removed, and will send Cogan thither unless a more important duty be found for him. Wheeler is being dispatched to Persia, and Morley is to go to Bantam; Elder and Janson will remain at Surat; while Taylor will follow his master. Note the instructions concerning the return cargo of the <em>Discovery</em>, and detail the steps they are taking to obtain the goods required. If the money at Goa be recovered, they will have no difficulty in providing the cargo. The accounts <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-153" data-page="153" data-folio="99" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n152_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 99">[p. 99]</button> of the pinnaces were sent home in the <em>Jonas</em>, and others will follow. Francisco de Souza de Castro has left Damān for Goa, his three years of office having expired; he is now employed as ambassador to Achin, besides other well-deserved preferments. They keep up correspondence with his successor, but are not on such cordial terms with him as they were with Don Francisco. Father Reimão, who is rector of a petty college at Bandora<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-004" href="#sec-06-fn-004" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-004" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, has been summoned to Goa to attend a provincial council of his Order. Will probably send the goods of both to that city. Were much afflicted to find themselves accused by the Company of dishonesty in regard to the debt claimed by Masīh-uz-Zamān; if this is meant in earnest, they wonder that they are not all recalled as unworthy of trust. Explain that when the junks were seized in 1623, the Hakīm, in order to recover more easily the money then taken from him, pretended that it belonged to Saif Khān; and the latter, in the following year, forced the English to give bills of exchange on Agra for the amount. However, on payment being tendered, the Hakīm refused to take it, owing to scruples of conscience. ‘It may well seeme very straunge that it should bee enforced from you 12 yeares and 5 monthes after; yet this is not more straunge then true. That hee receaved it not long before proceeded undoubtedly from scruple of conscience when hee was newly returned from Mocha, sanctified by the addition of Hadgee [Hājjī], attayned by holy pilgrimage to Mahometts shrine; which having now laid aside in the execution of accustomed juryes and oppressions proper to all Governors, hee challenged this money.’ The immediate cause of his so doing was the <em>William</em> landing her passengers at Dābhol instead of at Swally,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-005" href="#sec-06-fn-005" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-005" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> which exceedingly incensed him and caused him to vow that he would keep the factors in prison until the amount was paid. They satisfied his claim, not in order to obtain their freedom (for that the Company would not value at a quarter of the sum: ‘servants so much suspected are not woorth your redemption’), but because the demand appeared to be justified and because it was advisable to content him in view of the claims expected on account of the Diu junks. They must acknowledge that in this respect the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-154" data-page="154" data-folio="100" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n153_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 100">[p. 100]</button> Governor showed them abundant favour. As the Company most unjustly suspect them in this matter, they solemnly declare that none of them derived any personal profit from the transaction. That the Governor did not demand interest on the money was due to such action being forbidden by his religion, and 'this man, having bine twice at Mocha [<em>should be</em> Mecca], praetendeth to have somewhat of the saint in him'; yet his scruples did not prevent his insisting upon 215¼ rupees per 100 rials, with four per cent. allowance for 'vatawe<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-006" href="#sec-06-fn-006" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-006" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>'. 'Hee is just now outed of his goverment by Mezer Mulcke<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-007" href="#sec-06-fn-007" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-007" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, whom hee also wrought out and succeeded by farming Surratt at a greater rent; and now wee are resolved to complaine against him, wee feare without redresse, because wee have seldome seene any thing of this kind recovered. You have the certaine way<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-008" href="#sec-06-fn-008" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-008" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> in your owne power, and wee have offerd ourselves to bee ingaged in the execution, notwithstanding the knowne troubles which ensued to some of our praedecessors in the like case.' As regards the jeweller 'Sentidas<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-009" href="#sec-06-fn-009" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-009" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup>', their former letters advised the satisfaction of his demands, by which they freed themselves from a great deal of annoyance which his influence at court might have brought upon them. They have heard nothing more of the claim of Saif Khān, and think that he was merely fathering the jeweller's demand. Trust that all the troubles occasioned by the <em>Roebuck</em> are now ended, though they have been pursued on this account by 'a perverse Turke'. The difference between an amount mentioned in their letter and that shown in the accounts was due to a clerical error in the former. As the Company refuse to send scarlet or violet broadcloth for presents, they must use other things, for it is impossible to negotiate without making gifts. Further explanations regarding the defects in the indigo. Reply to the claim of Nathaniel Wyche. Thomas Reynolds was not detained on shore against his will by them, but by Death. In this Presidency they have of late lost only one man, viz. John Cape, who died in Ahmadābād on May 16. Having now answered the letter brought by the <em>Discovery</em>, they proceed to deal with that of October 13 [1637], of <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-155" data-page="155" data-folio="101" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n154_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 101">[p. 101]</button> which a copy was received at the same time as the former, and the original came afterwards to hand from Masulipatam. It gives a long account of the proceedings of the Dieppe man-of-war [<em>see</em> p. 60]; and this they have repeated to the Moors, whose incredulity has been reported already. The narrative may, however, assist them to recover the money at Goa. Regret the ill-success of the <em>Blessing</em>, but trust the Company have approved their action. The Persia factors complain without reason; the correspondence will show that no 'misbeseeming languadge' was used. Now proceed to narrate the disposure of the ships since the departure of the <em>Jonas</em>. As already advised, the <em>Michael</em> had been sent to 'Sinda, where Myre Sheriffe [<em>see</em> p. 33], the Mogulls embassadour to the Grand Signior, seized her for his transportation to Mocha'; whereupon the <em>Francis</em> was dispatched to the same port on February 18 [1638] to transport certain oil and butter lying there to Gombroon. Two days later the <em>Swan</em> left for Persia with a cargo of Deccan goods on account of the Company, besides freight to the value of 6,888 mahmūdīs. The <em>Blessing</em> reached Swally from Gombroon on March 3, and in her returned William Fremlen. Two days after the <em>Mary</em> came in from Armagon, having left that port on January 2. On March 9 the <em>Eagle</em> departed for Rājāpur. She is incapable of carrying any large quantity of goods, 'and therfore wee propose unto you rather the imployment of prammes [<em>see</em> p. 41] uppon these coasting voyadges, from which sort of vessells the Hollanders find good service.' On March 26 the <em>Mary</em> and <em>Blessing</em> sailed for Mokha. The former carried thither the goods she brought from Masulipatam, to which had been added Indian commodities to the value of 154,248 mahmūdīs 31¾ [pice], besides freight goods producing 4,372 mahmūdīs 20 pice; the cargo was entrusted to John Wylde and Edward Pearce. Except for a small quantity of cotton wool, the <em>Blessing</em> was entirely laden with freight goods, and yet the amount produced was only 24,768 mahmūdīs, 'notwithstanding that a corge deria<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-010" href="#sec-06-fn-010" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-010" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> which payeth but 8 m[ahmūdīs] to Gombroone paieth 5 rup[ees] to Mocha.' They taxed the ship's officers with having private trade on board, but this was denied. The <em>Eagle</em> returned from Rājāpur on April 17 with a freight of coco-nuts, and was then dispatched to Bantam on the 28th. Two days before the</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-156"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="156" data-folio="102" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n155_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 102">[p. 102]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Swan</em> had anchored at the river's mouth<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-011" href="#sec-06-fn-011" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-011" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>; she put ashore her passengers and 253 bales of silk and sailed for Masulipatam on May 3. At the request of the Dutch Director two of his factors were allowed to take passage in her. On May 2 'the taurim' [<em>see</em> p. 42] <em>Damān</em> returned from Goa [<em>should be Gombroon: see</em> p. 84], bringing six Persian horses and 120 bales of rūnās. Navigation then ceased on this coast till towards the end of August or beginning of September. Refer to the instructions and letters to factors for their designs in these voyages. Unscrupulous behaviour of the Mogul's ambassador. 'Having (paid and promised) a fraught of 4,000 rupees, the <em>Michaell</em> sett saile the 13th of February and arrived before Mocha the 16th of June; wheare the boates belonging to the towne being manned out brought in his Indian vessell which accompanied him (laden with a great part of his goods which the <em>Michaell</em> was not capable to receave), whilest hee (contrary to his promise and without the assistance of a pilott) enforced the <em>Michaell</em> forward in that unfraequented coorse unto Gidda; wheare by the way landing himself and the greatest part of her goods at a place called Ganipgahuras, hee promised to meete the shippe at Gidda; and, seeming to procure cammells to that purpose, hee cozened the port of its customes and posted to Mecha to performe his devotions for soe good successe; whilest the <em>Michaell</em>, endeavouring to meete him at Gidda according to his directions, passed some seene and many unknowne dangers with the losse of a cable and anchor; and yet beeing arrived, shee attended him fruitlesly, for hee came not, and soe avoided the payment of a remaynor of fraught agreed uppon to Mocha, an addition of fraught from Mocha to Gidda, the value of the cable and anchor lost in his service, and the gratuity which hee had promised to Mr. Proud for his care and trouble.' The vessel then returned to Mokha, where she obtained a freight to 'Zoar<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-013" href="#sec-06-fn-013" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-013" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> and Sinda', reaching 'Bandar Laree' on August 28. She sailed again on November 27 and arrived at Swally on the 15th current. The <em>Francis</em> did not get to 'Bandar Laree' until March 14; and, by the time she was laden, the winds were so strong that she could not get over the bar, and she was consequently obliged to winter there. She sailed for Gombroon on September 29, with <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-157" data-page="157" data-folio="103" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n156_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 103">[p. 103]</button> a cargo costing 20,355¾ rupees. The <em>Mary</em> reached Mokha on May 14, and found forty vessels in the road and the markets oppressed with abundance of goods and paucity of buyers, a great vessel from Suez having been wrecked on her way thither. Indian commodities were therefore exceedingly cheap; and the factors were obliged to bring back a considerable proportion of their piece-goods. Some of these are now sent to England; the rest must make a second voyage to the Red Sea. The sales at Mokha amounted to 47,379 rials of eight, 'which beeing returned in specie came properly to supply our urgent occations.' Great part of this money was derived from the sale of cloves, of which they were heartily glad to be rid, even at a low price; for on garbling one of the bales landed at Surat they found a large admixture of sticks and dust, 'besides the fassees<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-012" href="#sec-06-fn-012" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-012" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>.' Failing to sell the cloves here, they sent them to Ahmadābād, whence part was dispatched to Agra. The factors at Mokha were only required to pay three per cent. for customs, were well treated on shore, and were presented with a horse at departure. The <em>Mary</em> sailed thence on August 22 and reached Swally, together with the <em>Discovery</em>, on September 23. The <em>Blessing</em>, under Jacob Cottenshaw, on her outward voyage did not get to Aden till May 25; she then endeavoured to 'ply it upp' to Mokha, but with such ill success that her passengers all consented to her returning to Aden. An agreement was made to pay five per cent. in composition of all customs, presents, and bribes; and Richard Fitch, the purser, then landed with some cotton wool belonging to the Company, in lieu of which he sent on board a quantity of 'sagy<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-013-2" href="#sec-06-fn-013" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-013" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, which is a kind of salt especially used in this place in the making of soape'. He was, however, himself detained on shore, with three or four other Englishmen, for what reason is not known; and the <em>Blessing</em>, after waiting till August 26, set sail for India without them. Further particulars cannot be obtained until the ship returns from Rājāpur, where she arrived in safety on October 14. The <em>Eagle</em> reached Bantam on June 9. The <em>Swan</em> got to Armagon on May 29, whence, after landing her Dutch passengers, she proceeded to Masulipatam, arriving there June 4. She could, however, procure no freight or passengers, and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-158" data-page="158" data-folio="104" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n157_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 104">[p. 104]</button> left again for Gombroon on July 6 with some piece-goods and cloves only ; but, being delayed by contrary winds and fearing that she would not be able to return by the time desired, she put into Swally instead, arriving October 17. Refer to the book of consultations for the reasons why the <em>Mary</em> was not sent home last March.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><blockquote><p>‘ In the next place wee conceave it not improper to lett you see the causelesse troubles which have bine accumulated uppon us by a corrupt Governor, who, having favored us in the businesse of Dio (because hee foresawe the contrary would have bine our ruine of his port), expected that wee should have paid for his protection after the rate which wee knew some of our praedecessors contributed to Saiffe Ckawne ; and soe much hee made no scruple to communicate unto us ; whilest we, that stood supported by our innocence, alleadged that to bee a farr different case, for then (uppon just grounds) that which was seized remayned in our possession ; now wee have taken nothing, yet had bine most unjustly enforced to make restitution heere at Suratt and continued still to bee molested by the merchants of Amadavad and Cambaia ; whearin wee</p></blockquote></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">acknowledged hee had done us an especiall favor that hee had done us justice, or rather protected us against injustice; which wee conceaved wee had sufficiently gratified by the large and unaccustomed praesents which had bine given him for two or three years; together with the pardawe [<em>parda</em>, a curtain] which sometimes covered Mahometts shrine,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-014" href="#sec-06-fn-014" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-014" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> sent to the King by him at our charge. Besides, wee let him see how, the English Agent resident at Agra having intimated unto the King our innocency and consented unto the transportation of his embassadour for Turky, hee was pleased to pardon and protect us against all mens praetences (for soe much Mr. Borneford advised proceeded in publique from his owne mouth). But as the hunger of gold, like to that of meat, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-159" data-page="159" data-folio="105" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n158_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 105">[p. 105]</button> is not sattisfied with woords, soe our Governor, who had now disposed himself to enforce money from all sortes of men (hee cared not uppon what conditions), sought that in our praejudice which hee could not attayne unto whilest hee did us curtesyes. It is the custome of this country, for all sommes recovered by the Governors assistance, to allow unto him a fourth part. If it happen by virtue of the Kings firmaen, the King hath it, or soe much more as the praetenders make offer unto him; as appeared in those of Dio, who first gave the King a third, then the halfe, of what should bee enforced from us. From this custome it is not improbable that our Governor expected from us a fourth at least of that which the firmaen enordered to bee paid; which hee thought wee should pay for his protection that interposed himself in our favor against the positive command of the King [<em>see the previous volume</em>, p. 313]; and therfore, failing in his first projects, hee resolved <em>per fas aut nefas</em> hee would one way or other make sure of his share.’ An oppor-</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">tunity was afforded by the arrival of the <em>Blessing</em> from Persia, when certain of her passengers complained that they had been robbed of ‘zerbaffes’ [<em>zarbāf</em>, cloth of gold] and other valuable commodities. ‘Wee were convented before him, who beganne with badd languadge and, before hee would heare what wee could say, hee commanded us to make sattisfaccion.’ Fremlen, however, was able to produce tickets signed at Gombroon by the claimants, acknowledging that their bales contained only rosewater, fruit, &amp;c. The merchants admitted that they had described their goods falsely, but said that this was done in order to save customs and freight; to which it was retorted that the English received no part of the customs on exports, and charged freight by bulk, not by value. Then the complainants offered to swear that their goods were stolen aboard the ship; whereupon the Governor said that reparation must be made by the English, unless they would make oath to the contrary. Accordingly Fremlen solemnly swore upon the Evangelists that the goods were not, so far as he knew, purloined on board the ship; ‘and this served the turne at that time, after they had added many particular reproaches to the generall scandall which they imposed uppon our nation.’ Neither side was disposed to give anything to the Governor, who was therefore forced to wait for another opportunity. The <em>Swan</em> was the next ship to arrive from <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-160" data-page="160" data-folio="106" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n159_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 106">[p. 106]</button> Gombroon, and two Armenians who came in her lodged complaints; one had lost his ‘buckshaw’ [<em>bukchah</em>, a bundle], containing articles valued at 146 mahmūdīs, while the other had had his ‘sapetto’ [<em>see</em> p. 53] cut open and some amber beads stolen. There being no time to search the ship, the Armenians were reimbursed and the ship’s company ordered to make good the loss. Some days after the departure of the <em>Swan</em>, complaints were lodged by other passengers and the factors were again summoned before the Governor, who loaded them with reproaches. They produced the freight list, showing that no valuable goods, such as were claimed, had been shipped; and they suggested that the missing articles might have been stolen either by the camelmen in Persia or by the boatmen who carried them to and from the ships. Further, they pointed out that the bales had been lying for some days in the customhouse, which was known to have been broken open and some things carried off. As before, the claimants offered to swear that the thefts had been committed by the English; ‘from whence ensued the positive doome of our corrupt Governor, which was that the Muselmans oath must bee taken, and wee must pay the money. And when wee offerd to depose the contrary, in such manner as had bine formerly done, hee reviled us bitterly in having exceeded the rules of our religion, which (saith hee) in the expresse woords of Christ in the Gospell forbiddeth a Christian to sweare at all, which before was permitted by Moses to the Jewes. And thus hee preacheth to his auditory, who magnify his great reading and knowledge of all religions; whearunto when wee replied that which displeased him, hee fell uppon us with the <em>Roebucks</em> depredations, and what hee had done for us; and then hee produced the Kings firmaens, which enordered the seizure of that which was praetended to bee taken from the shippes of Dio, relating his interposition; and now againe his parasites magnify his abundant favors, and revile us that had praesented no more thankfullness.’ On the English protesting their innocence, ‘hee affirmed that the <em>Roebucke</em> returned for England, and that the King had taken into his possession that which shee had acquired in her piraticall proceedings,’ adding that he had been told this by ‘Allee Ballee’, the Persian ambassador. He then gave judgement that, upon the merchants taking their oaths before the ‘Cazy’ [<em>Qāzī</em>, judge], the English must make <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-161" data-page="161" data-folio="107" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n160_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 107">[p. 107]</button> good the amounts claimed. ‘Soe wee departed, resolved to have opposed him even to imprisonment’; but they changed their minds when they considered that in such case ‘it would bee divulged that the English nation were immpresonnid for theft’, that they were quite at the Governor’s mercy, and that some of the goods at least were known to have been stolen on board. However, ‘wee resolved to stand the shocke of theire clamors untill it came to vyolence; wherunto they suddenly brought it, for the Governor, conventing us, told us plainely that wee must pay or bee impprisonned; whearunto when wee seemed to submitt and found him to bee in good earnest, wee protested against the injustice and (with that reluctancy which the cause required) wee seemed to bee induced by the mediation of the merchants of the towne; and soe accompanying our adversaryes to the Cazy, wee lookt on whilest the Moores tooke theire oathes uppon the Alcoran [<em>al-Qurān</em>]; and then theare was no remedy but to pay downe the money in the same place; when wee could not avoide the losse of 11,585 m[ahmūdīs]. The Armenians tooke theire oathes uppon the Testament in the hands of our minister, Mr. Theodore Holditch.’ Wrote overland to have the <em>Swan</em> searched for the stolen goods on her arrival at Masulipatam; but her master, Thomas Steevens, had already done this on the way, and had found some of the goods in the possession of Thomas Gattaker, who had been punished before for other delinquencies. Nothing more was discovered; ‘soe that undoubtedly wee have bine grossely abused.’ Debated whether they would not refuse to take goods on freight in future; but found this impossible without submitting to greater inconveniences at the hands of the Governors of Surat and Gombroon. However, they have decided only to embark such goods at the latter place after a declaration of their nature has been signed by the owner, who will also be required to examine his goods before they leave the ship, after which no complaints will be entertained. Advised in their previous letters of the trouble caused by a Turk who pretended that he had been robbed by the English, and brought a letter from ‘an eminent nobleman residing at court’ requesting the Governor to procure him satisfaction. That was easily answered, as the Governor was then in expectation of a large gratuity from the English. The Turk, however, returned to Agra <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-162" data-page="162" data-folio="108" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n161_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 108">[p. 108]</button> and procured the King’s farmān, ‘which peremptorily commanded hee should bee sattisfied from the English to the value of 30,000 rupees, and rebuked the Governor as too much inclining to our favor; and yet hee leaves it to his judgment whither or noe the Turkes demaunds bee just, or the English lyable to pay.’ Their correspondence with Agra and other places will show what troubles this caused for a period of five months, and that in spite of the Governor they remained firm in their resolution not to part with any money. ‘Wee rather offered ourselves to appeare before the King in person, and soe in conclusion wee became obliged by a writing under our hands; whilest, Mr. Bornford having negotiated a counter firmaund, that businesse is taken upp for the time. Yet wee feare it is not soe ended, for the Turke is indefatigably clamorous and resolved to renue his suite; to which purpose hee is againe departed towards the court.’ Refer to their correspondence with other factories for ‘particular passadges of your whole affaires’, which are omitted now for fear of making the letter too long.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘Amongst other thinges you will find fraequent complaints of our Governors most impetuous proceedings against all sortes of people subsisting under his command, and how farre his rapine had bine extended against Virgee Vorah and allmost all that had any thing to loose; in which nomber our brokers and moody [<em>modī</em>, steward] most unjustly suffered because they were our servants, and wee because wee had not sufficiently gratified him for protecting us against our adversaryes of Dio.’ ‘In this praedicament wee continued untill the arrivall of the <em>Discovery</em>, when, your President taking leave to repaire to Swally and soe aboord according to custome, the Governor would not consent, producing the Kings firmaen, which, amongst other thraldomes that hee had inserted and procured to bee firmed, prohibeteth the principalls of the English and Dutch nations to moove out of Suratt untill released by theire successors they shall returne to theire native country. The same firmaen enjoyneth that 12 laackes of rup[ee]s should bee allwaies deposited in his kingdome, that the shippes should allwayes ride in Swally Hole, and that no friggatts should bee built in India: all soe unreasonable that wee publiquely profest wee could not submitt unto such unaequall conditions, and therfore about three yeares since, when the firmaen was first published, wee declared <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-163" data-page="163" data-folio="109" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n162_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 109">[p. 109]</button> ourselves opposers; soe that from that tyme to this present it was sometimes spoken of but never urged to our observance. To conclude, after wee had mooved him diverse times by ourselves and freinds and found him inexorable, wee tooke leave that William Fremlen &amp;c. might repaire aboord; which beeing permitted, and the <em>Mary</em> from Mocha arrived at the same time with the <em>Discovery</em>, wee resolved to make a businesse of it, and then enordered that the passengers and theire treasure which came uppon her should bee detayned on boord with respective usadge untill wee had negotiated our former liberty which by agreement was graunted unto us when the trade was first setled and since that time confirmed by a treaty with Saiffe Ckawne, braunched foorth into divers articles, one whearof permitted the Presidents repaire aboord at his pleasure, having first acquainted the Governor for the time beeing and taking his leave in testimony of respect [<em>see the 1624–29 volume</em>, p. 29]. It would appeare impertinently troublesome to insert in this place the diversified conference which ensued uppon this occation. The Governor continued obstinate in receding nothing from his intendments; wee on the other side would not consent to captive our nation and annihilate our agreements; whilest the passengers that were on boord and the merchants that were interressed in the treasure interposed themselves to accomodate a faire conclusion, which after 10 dayes time ended the difference. The Governor gave us a writing, which the principall merchants of the towne ingaged themselves to see accomplished; and then, having first released the <em>Maryes</em> passengers, that the Governor might have the honor of the day, our silke was licenced to bee sent aboord, and William Methwold had leave to goe downe to Swally. Soe that all ended in a seeming calme; yet wee forbare to land our goods and monyes untill our former treaty should bee confirmed; whearin wee praevalied, and then proceeded as before without any interruption. And heere ... wee put a period to this businesse and Hackymes government. For the cry of the oppressed generallity arriving to the Kings knowledg by the Duuans [Dīwān's] advises, who differing with the Governor disapproved his cruell coorses, Mier Moza or, as hee was last named, Mezer Mulcke, is againe admitted to the government, Virgee Vorah sent for to court to answer in person, and by the Kings owne nomination a qualified <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-164" data-page="164" data-folio="110" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n163_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 110">[p. 110]</button> person companyes Mezer Mulcke to Suratt to take information of all mens aggreivances that they may bee redressed; whilest in the meane time the assignes of Mezer Mulcke officiate all places of government; for which (as it is reported) hee giveth two laacks of rupias [<em>but see</em> p. 123] more then his praedecessor.' Having ordered an investment of Malabar pepper and other Deccan goods, and Vīrjī Vōra being unable (owing to his imprisonment by the Governor) to fulfil his engagement in connexion therewith, they had no option but to send a vessel to Rājāpur to fetch away the goods. The <em>Kit</em> sailed for this purpose on September 26 and reached her post, after some skirmishes with Malabar frigates, on October 4; on the return voyage she called at Damān for some arrack, and got back to Surat on December 22. The 'taurim' [<em>see</em> p. 41] bought of the Captain of Damān was during the rains made into a handsome vessel, and is now called the <em>Diamond</em>; she was dispatched to Gombroon on November 7 with a cargo of cloves, tobacco, and sandal-wood. The <em>Discovery</em> sailed for the same destination on December 22, carrying goods on the Company's account valued at 41,777 mahmūdīs, together with freight goods producing 20,611 mahmūdīs. With her went the <em>Convoy</em>, laden with the turmeric and 'moretotoo' [<em>see</em> p. 87] received from Rājāpur. She has instructions to remain at Gombroon until the end of the monsoon, to assist in lading and unlading the various ships, in lieu of the country boats, which are expensive and 'extreamly theevish'. The only vessels now at Swally are the <em>Mary</em>, <em>Blessing</em>, <em>Swan</em>, and <em>Michael</em>. Want of weight in the sugar brought from Masulipatam and Bantam. The silk received from the <em>Swan</em> sustained some damage during the rains. The calicoes bought here are good, but somewhat dear on account of the number of competing buyers. The aloes Socotrina is sent because it is both good and cheap. On the advice of Captain Minors it has been taken out of the skins and put into casks. Return Sir Francis Crane's tapestry depicting the story of Vulcan and Venus, for which they cannot find a customer. At Agra Āsaf Khān offered a price so far short of its owner's valuation that Bornford refused to accept it. As regards the Rājā's debt [<em>see the previous volume</em>, p. 54], Bornford writes that, notwithstanding the King's farmān for its payment, he is of opinion that it will not be recovered unless a good part of it be allowed 'to <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-165" data-page="165" data-folio="111" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n164_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 111">[p. 111]</button> a powerfull undertaker’. The Ahmadābād indigo is poorer than usual and dearer than any before sent home; but this is due to the competition of the Dutch, who bought 2,800 bales and thus forced up the price. The Governor of Ahmadābād obliged both the English and the Dutch to buy a small quantity belonging to his wife. The cotton yarn is mostly a remainder left over from the <em>Jonas</em>. Could not get more sugar in time, and therefore bought dry ginger, sugar candy, and ‘noshadar’ [<em>see</em> p. 134], instead. Have also sent some gum-lac provided in Agra. In lading the <em>Mary</em> the Malabar pepper has been laid at the bottom of the hold, and ‘takes only that place which must have bine otherwise supplied with stones’; this will help to countervail its dearness. She is to call at Goa in order that Methwold may solicit the restitution of the money there laid under embargo. The <em>Swan</em> will wait until January 10 for the arrival of the <em>Comfort</em> and, failing her appearance, will then embark the goods at Swally and endeavour to overtake the <em>Mary</em> at the Cape; if not, the two are to wait for each other at St. Helena until May 10. A great loss has been sustained owing to most of the <em>Michael’s</em> lading being spoiled by the water which had leaked into her. On December 22 the <em>Francis</em> arrived from Gombroon with 243 bales of silk and 241 of rūnās. Part of the silk was put into the <em>Mary</em>, and the rest reserved for the <em>Swan</em>. Send copies of the letters received from Persia. Heard from Masulipatam that the <em>Comfort</em>, which was dispatched from Bantam to Surat at the end of last July with a cargo of pepper, sugar, &amp;c., had been driven to the northwards and forced into Masulipatam, which was reached on September 26. As soon as her wants were supplied she was to sail for this port. If she fails to arrive before the departure of the <em>Swan</em>, she must go on to Gombroon, where her goods will sell to considerable profit and ‘releive in some measure our dejected condition’. The few factors left at Masulipatam have neither means nor credit. They dispatched the <em>Thomas</em> towards Gombroon in February [1638] with cloves and sugar, besides freight goods and passengers; but she failed to attain her port and was obliged to winter at ‘a place of the Portugalls called Tutacurrin’ [Tuticorin]. She sailed thence on September 15, and plying up the Malabar Coast found the <em>Blessing</em> at Rājāpur. ‘By the <em>Jonah</em> wee advised as much <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-166" data-page="166" data-folio="112" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n165_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 112">[p. 112]</button> as was made knowne to us from the Portugalls and Hollanders concerning Captaine Weddalls proceedings; but both were mistaken in theire reports of his death. It is true hee proceeded to Macaw, having first setled a factory at Acheene, wheare Edward Knipe was cheife merchant. Arrived at Macaw and desiring commerce, the Portugalls denied it for want of the Viceroyes warrant; from which resolution when they could not bee remooved, Captaine Weddall found meanes to get pilates to conduct him into the River of Canton. And theare having treated with some Chyneses concerning trade, Nathaniell Mountney, his brother, and some others, together with an important part of theire cargazoone, were landed in some place allotted unto them. And theire they commenced a trade which produced in a short time about 60 or 70 tonnes of goods, which were presently sent on boord; whilest the Chyneses, that now thought them confident, praepared fire shippes with a purpose to ruine them; but they were discovered and praevented. And now beganne publique acts of hostility; for Captaine Weddall, understanding of certaine junckes which roade therabouts, hee made meanes to fire them; and withall hee tooke from the Chyneses a small fortification, whilest his merchants with the remaynor of theire stocke wer kept prisonners in Canton. In fine, when no other remedy could bee found, hee applied himself to the Portugalls; and, making use of theire moderation, hee recovered his merchants after more then three monthes imprisonment. And receaving for the money which was amongst them such goods as they were pleased to deliver, hee sould his <em>Anne</em> at Macao, and with the <em>Dragon</em>, <em>Sunne</em>, and <em>Katharine</em>, having receaved on boord the <em>Dragon</em> Portugalls and theire goods to bee landed at Cochyne, hee past the Straights of Malacca, wheare hee found the Dutch, which seemed to oppose him, but they were better advised; soe that after some bravadoes they parted freinds.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-015" href="#sec-06-fn-015" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-015" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> And then Captaine Weddall, dispeeding the <em>Katharyne</em> to Battecala,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-016" href="#sec-06-fn-016" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-016" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> wheare hee hoped to have fullfilled her lading with pepper, the <em>Dragon</em> and <em>Sunne</em> repaired to Acheene, from whence the <em>Sunne</em> sett saile <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-167" data-page="167" data-folio="113" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n166_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 113">[p. 113]</button> for England some time in March and the <em>Dragon</em> proceeded to Cochyne; wheare having landed his charge and receaved his fraught, his <em>Katharine</em> came to him frustrated of that shee went to seeke, for theare was neither pepper, money, nor men at Battecala.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-017" href="#sec-06-fn-017" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-017" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Soe that now they were put uppon new resolutions. The <em>Katharine</em> could not goe home for want of lading, nor could either of [<em>sic</em>] both winter uppon that coast with safety; insomuch that Mesulapatam is now made the rendevouze, and thether they sayled (having attempted trade at Negapatnam, but in vaine), and they arrived the 29th [19th?] of May. Your Agent, Thomas Clarke, having notice of his old acquaintance newly arrived, found meanes to gett aboord, and theare (for want of witt, spirritt, or fidelity) they contrive together how Captaine Weddall may bee supplied with lading to his most advantage; to which purpose least hee should pay custome, both inwards and outwards, they resolve to affirme themselves to bee all one Company; and, that it may appeare to bee soe, Clarke hee furnisheth of his factors to bee joyned with those of Captaine Weddall in following the investments at Petepoley, Ve[r]acheroane, &amp;c. And now they conceave they dance in a nett safe enough from the Governors discovery. But the Dutch (who were no strangers to Captaine Weddalls proceedings), beeing vexed with such new competitors, concealed nothing which they knew; insomuch that the Governor required custome; which the new English resolutely opposed, and thus some time was spent whilest the Kings pleasure was attended from above, unto whom the Governor praetended to advise the point in difference; whearin hee dealt soe effectually for himself that Captaine Weddall was commanded to pay custome or depart the port. And then it was agreed that hee should pay 2½ per cent., afterwards 3½, and then 4; uppon which alterations theare ensued <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-168" data-page="168" data-folio="114" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n167_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 114">[p. 114]</button> so much discontent that, Captaine Weddall imbarquing his merchants and goods and to that purpose making use of one of the country boates, hee placed certaine ordenance uppon her and, soe entring the river as high as the towne, hee made divers shott, which slew one man and hurt more. But when hee understood that the Governor went about to hinder his going by sinking a vessell at the rivers mouth, hee hastened aboord and remayned at distance, whilest the Governor fortefies at the rivers mouth and sinkes all the towne boates, least the English should make use of them, theire owne not beeing serviceable to plant ordenance uppon. Yet after all this there was a seeming reconciliation; but it continued not long, untill the Governor, finding divers of his [Weddell’s] men at the barre mending theire sailes, hee seizeth and carrieth them a few miles into the country. Others that were at the house, amongst which was Mr. Hatch and Mr. John Carter, hee caused theare to bee detayned; but under soe negligent a guard that they made an escape and, foording the river, came to the seaside; wheare making a signe to the shippes, they were fetcht aboord and gayned theire liberty. But after all this, peace was soe necessary to both parties that, by the mediation of the President for the Danes, all thinges were salved; the Governor had his desires in receaving his demands of customes; certaine vessells bound for Bengala were released; the English had theire men and as many goods aboord as amounted to 5,000 pagodes; and soe Captaine Weddall with his <em>Dragon</em> and <em>Katharine</em> set saile from his troublesome port of Mesulapatam the 21th of September, intending his coorse for Batacola, wheare wee conceave hee expecteth to meete supply out of England.’ While at Masulipatam he sent a messenger to Surat to fetch any letters that might have arrived for him. In reply the letter which came in the <em>Jonas</em> was forwarded. Another (conceived to be of the same tenor), which had been entrusted privately to Mr. Shilling and was found after his death, was dispatched thither as soon as news came of Weddell’s arrival there; ‘at which time wee wrott besides that which wee thought fitting concerning theire intrusion into the places of your trade; but they would never vouchsafe an answer; nor indeed your Agent theare, at least to any purpose; insomuch that the most which wee have heere inserted wee have receaved from the Dutch. And now you may bee well assured that, if Captaine <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-169" data-page="169" data-folio="115" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n168_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 115">[p. 115]</button> Weddall hath aggreived them in any thing for which they have not receaved full sattisfaction, your servants and estates will suffer for it ; for soe Thomas Rogers, in a letter of his dated the 19th November, answereth our demaunds of newes concerning Captaine Weddalls proceedings in these woords : " How Captaine Weddall proceedes I am ignorant alltogether, for I have had noe advise therof; yet I can tell you that I was in fast hold about 50 odd dayes for his accions, eeven untill hee departed off this coast."' The <em>Planter</em> was seen before Goa in October, and proceeded thence to Bhatkal, where (according to the Portuguese) she found 'but Lenten entertainment ; for the King, understanding the Viceroyes dislike, refused to admitt them entrance'.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-018" href="#sec-06-fn-018" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-018" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> It is said that the <em>Planter</em> thereupon sailed to seek Weddell on the Coast of Coromandel. Probably further intelligence will be obtained by the <em>Mary</em> when she calls at Goa. 'The Portugalls story wee concluded the last yeare with the relation of theire successe in theire fight with the Hollanders ; after which time they were content to sitt still, notwithstanding that the Dutch continued before theire barre untill the halfe of Aprill ; wheare they enjoyed the content of seeing two of theire enemyes shippes burnt through misfortune, negligence, or treachery. The first whearof was a carrack<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-019" href="#sec-06-fn-019" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-019" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, which from a candle tooke fire and consumed past any remedy that could bee used ; the other a galleoon, called the <em>Madre de Deos</em>, whose captaine with some of his most respected freinds beeing that night landed, to the number of 15 persons, about midnight the shipp blew upp ;<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-020" href="#sec-06-fn-020" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-020" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> wheare more then 300 soules 'perished at once, no man knowing by what accident the powder should take fire. The other carrack<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-021" href="#sec-06-fn-021" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-021" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> (the same which arrived last) sett saile from Goa the 26th of September, laden for Lisbon and speeded soe early least the Hollanders should have arrived and interrupted her. The [   ] of October two galleons arrived at Goa,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-022" href="#sec-06-fn-022" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-022" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup> the Hollanders <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-170" data-page="170" data-folio="116" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n169_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 116">[p. 116]</button> fleete, consisting of 11 saile, beeing then at Vingerla (some of them in sight, but could not hinder theire safe arrivall). Soe that now the Portugalls beeing reinforct with a new galleon finished lately at Goa and these two new arrived, the greater wherof carrieth 60 peices of brasse ordenance, they give out that they will give the Hollanders battaile, and Christmas Day is designed to bee the time. What concerneth our businesse depending theare, William Thurston (imployed thearin) returned nothing but good woords, and withall faire hopes and promises that when William Methwould shall come to demaund it, it shall certainly bee delivered unto him, that soe hee may depart in peace. Wee have the same renued by letters now lately receaved. God grant it may proove true; for then it will bee woorth soe much deviation. The Hollanders, that for some yeares last past carried the whole trade before them in these partes of India and Persia, have soe overacted theire severall partes (that wee might bee ingaged in the like inconveniencies) that necessity at this time makes them quiett enough, if our like praedicament would permitt us to make good use of theire necessity.' The Dutch have received such a quantity of Persian silk at Batavia that they have sent 500 bales to Japan for sale; and they have a large stock of indigo here, part of which is to be taken to Persia to procure funds to pay off their immense debt in India. The <em>Bredam</em> was to have carried this indigo and other goods to Batavia last year, but she was detained at Gombroon by a dispute over a Portuguese vessel which she had captured laden with goods belonging to Indian merchants of Tatta, and so she was unable to call at Surat. The <em>'sHertogenbosch</em> was thereupon sent from Batavia on July 20, 1638, to fetch the said goods; but she did not reach this place until October 14, and it being then too late to return by the date fixed, she was dispatched to Gombroon instead, sailing on December 21, with freight goods producing about 50,000 mahmūdīs. Details of the commodities she brought hither, with the prices demanded and offered; most of them will be sent to Agra and Ahmadābād, as prices are low here. The Dutch are reported to be indebted in India to the extent of eight lakhs of rupees, for which they are paying interest at 1¼ and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-171" data-page="171" data-folio="117" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n170_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 117">[p. 117]</button> 1½ per cent. per month; but they expect shortly two vessels from China bringing goods to clear this liability. Their fleet designed for Goa<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-023" href="#sec-06-fn-023" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-023" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> sailed some days before the <em>'sHertogenbosch</em>, but was delayed in the same manner by calms and contrary winds, and lost 130 men in the voyage, besides nearly 400 sick at the time of their arrival in October. They refreshed at Dābhol and are now blockading Goa. ‘Two smaller shippes of theires (such as they call jachts), intended to Xeloan [Ceylon] to theire late conquest Matecalo [<em>see</em> p. 73], beeing driven to leeward and soe enforced to Pallecat, found theare such tempestuous weather the 6th of October last that they were driven on shoare, wheare the shippes, goods, and maney of the persons perished.’ Adverting now to their own situation, they forward papers showing the number of factors employed here, and the stock available. In lading the <em>Discovery</em> they will carefully follow the directions of the Company. Arrival of the <em>Francis</em>. She left Gombroon on November 1 with 243 bales of silk and 241 of rūnās, and reached Swally December 22, after a very tedious passage. Some of the silk has been damaged by wet. Remarks on the new contract made by the Persia factors with the Shāh, their complaints that ‘wee deale too roughly with them’, and their proceedings regarding a bill of exchange [<em>see</em> pp. 84, 86]. ‘There is such a fatalety in that Persian monarchy as transports all of our nation as soone as they are transplanted.’ Even the present Agent, who left Gombroon with a determination to ‘practize the comportment of a merchant’, has now taken up a different attitude. Methwold has availed himself of the permission given in the letters received by the <em>Jonas</em> to resign his position, and now goes home in the <em>Mary</em>. Robert Manly returns in the same ship; also Thomas Ditchfield, who came out with Methwold as an attendant. He has served some years at 20<em>l.</em> per annum, and hopes for further employment. Edward Elcock, ‘having expired his covenants,’ and been relieved by Richard Vincent, takes passage in the <em>Mary</em>; ‘he is a very able man in his quality.’ ‘George Oxinden, though yet a youth, hath served you six yeares in India and hath attayned to perfection in the Indestan languadge. Wee found him heere, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-172" data-page="172" data-folio="118" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n171_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 118">[p. 118]</button> brought on shoare by Mr. Arthur Hatch<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-024" href="#sec-06-fn-024" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-024" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, minister, and heere hee hath continued untill now, imployed for the most part in Amadavad, wheare hee hath bine serviceable uppon all occations. Hee is of good parentage, whom in duty hee desires to visitt; but wee beleive his greatest discouragement is that his wages is but 10<em>s.</em> per month; which although wee see cause enough to increase, yet because wee are sollicited by him that hee may returne, wee present him to your better esteeme; for besides that hee hath languadge to deserve it, wee professe hee is free from all vice as farr as ever wee have seene or heard. Theare is a young gentleman named John Albrecht Van Manslo, who accompanied the Duke of Holstanes embassador by the Caspian Sea to the King of Persia. When the embassadour returned, this gentleman, whither out of curiosity or discontent, made choice to come for India. And arriving heere with the <em>Swanne</em> about the end of Aprill, hee hath lived ever since amongst us, the civillest, modestest, and fairest behaved that wee have ever knowne of his age and education. Hee declareth that it was his purpose to seeke a passadge by sea, which hee hoped to have found at his first arrivall. Wee made litle difficulty to grant it him; but withall wee declared ourselves to bee servants, and therfore left him accomptable to you for the dyett and passadge of himselfe and servant; whearunto hee willingly submitted.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-025" href="#sec-06-fn-025" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-025" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> Hee praetendeth to have some neare relation to the Duke of Holsteine, as having bine sometimes his page, as hee had bine to Count Walleisteine, Duke of Freedland.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-026" href="#sec-06-fn-026" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-026" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> You may please to approve of this our accomodation of him, since in truth wee conceave hee doth well deserve it.’ The <em>Blessing</em>, being unable to ‘subsist’ for want of men, sails, and cordage, will either be sold at Goa or laid up. Her ordnance has been distributed already among the other ships. The <em>Michael</em> will go with her and the <em>Mary</em> to Goa, in order to bring back the crew of the <em>Blessing</em> and also <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-173" data-page="173" data-folio="119" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n172_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 119">[p. 119]</button> Andrew Cogan and John Wylde, who are sent to assist in the recovery of the money and the disposal of the ship. They will further, 'if it shall bee soe required,' treat with the Portuguese regarding the trade of China. On her return the <em>Michael</em> will lade for Mokha. The <em>Swan</em> is waiting for the pepper expected in the <em>Comfort</em>; she will then depart about the middle of January, and go by way of the channel between Madagascar and the mainland. About the same time the <em>Francis</em> will sail for Gombroon. The <em>Diamond</em>, which left here on November 7, is expected back in time to be dispeeded again before the end of January. She will possibly be instructed to 'take Tatha in her way'; at least if news shall come of the arrival there of Philip Wylde, 'who was sent from Agra to Lahore to discover the conveniency of the river of Indus unto the port of Bandar Laree.' On her return she must winter in the river of Surat. The <em>Discovery</em>, which sailed on December 21, will, it is hoped, be back before the end of February; and then, 'if better businesse from Goa doth not divert,' they intend to dispatch her to Mokha with freight goods. 'Theare have bine lately recomended unto us from Agra two disstressed Christian travellers who, deluded with hopes of India, found meanes to travell thither overland; but, frustrate of theire expectation, they know no other way home then that which in charity wee now affoord them. One is an Italian of Turin, named Joseph Teigee, who praetends to have had good correspondence with some English merchants residing in Marcellis and Aleppo and to have performed good offices for them uppon occation. Hee hath by himself and freinds importuned to bee admitted <em>in forma pauperis</em>, which wee have graunted for Gods sake, because wee cannot forgett ourselves also to bee strangers in a forraigne land. The other, named Arnold Bresson, is a Frenchman, whom at your charge wee thus redeeme from the misery which might befall him in beeing distrest amongst a perverse generation of Mahumetans and heathens.' (56½ <em>pp. Received December 17, 1639.</em>)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Enclosure:</em> Invoice of the goods laden on the <em>Mary</em> and <em>Swan</em>, consisting of Persian silk; Coast and Bengal goods (calicoes, sugar, indigo, and saltpetre); indigo and saltpetre from Agra; piece-goods from Broach, Baroda, and Ahmadābād; saltpetre, cotton yarn, indigo, and ginger from Ahmadābād; cotton wool; sugar (from <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-174" data-page="174" data-folio="120" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n173_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 120">[p. 120]</button> Bantam); gum-lac; aloes Socotrina; pepper (from the Deccan); and some beads for barter on the way home. Total, 2,788 bales, costing 1,375,231 mahmūdīs 5 pice. Also one bale of Crane's tapestry returned, invoiced at 30,000 mahmūdīs. The charges are set out in great detail. The rupee is reckoned at 2½ mahmūdīs; the Masulipatam pagoda at 10, and the Armagon pagoda at 8, mahmūdīs; 3⅕ shāhīs go to the mahmūdī; and 16 lārīs are taken as equivalent to 13 mahmūdīs. (17 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Bill of Lading of the</span> <em>MARY</em>, <span class="sc">January</span> 4, 1639 (<em>Marine Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. iv. no. 17).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Specifying the number of bales of the various commodities. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">William Bayley's Account of the Homeward Voyage of the</span> <em>MARY</em><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-027" href="#sec-06-fn-027" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-027" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> (<em>Marine Records</em>, vol. lxii. p. 107).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">1639, <em>January</em> 5. Sailed. Met the <em>Michael</em>, which departed the same evening for Rājāpur. <em>January</em> 7. Anchored at Bassein, where they found the <em>Blessing</em>. <em>January</em> 8. Both ships departed. <em>January</em> 10. Anchored off Goa. <em>January</em> 11. ‘This morning came aboard us the Genirall and the Vize-Admirall of the fleete, with divers other gentlemen from the fleete and cittie. About ten aclock the President &amp;c. went ashore with the Genirall, and soe to the cittie of Goa, where they were kindlie entertayned.’ <em>January</em> 22. Sailed for Cannanore, ‘to releeve those English captives as were of the <em>Comfort</em>, taken by the Mallabarrs.’ <em>January</em> 25. Anchored at Cannanore, where they found Captain Weddell with the <em>Dragon</em>, <em>Katherine</em>, and <em>Planter</em>. <em>January</em> 26. Repaid Weddell 2,200 rials of eight, which he had spent in redeeming the master and fourteen men of the <em>Comfort</em> from the Malabars. <em>January</em> 27. Sailed. <em>January</em> 30. Seventeen Malabar frigates attacked the <em>Mary</em>, but were repulsed. <em>February</em> 4. Passed Cape Comorin. <em>March</em> 21. Fire in the steward's room. <em>March</em> 25. Tried their two stills, and found that in 24 hours they could distill 44 or 46 gallons of fresh water, ‘drinckable if necessity should inforce’. <em>March</em> 27. Thomas Lucas put into irons.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-028" href="#sec-06-fn-028" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-028" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> At a general consultation it was <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-175" data-page="175" data-folio="121" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n174_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 121">[p. 121]</button> decided to make for Mauritius to wood and water. <em>March</em> 28.
