Sighted Rodriguez. Another consultation held, at which it was unanimously decided to go direct to the Cape of Good Hope. <em>April</em> 5. Passed Cape Sebastian, on Madagascar. <em>April</em> 28. Saw the African coast. <em>May</em> 1. Cape Falso sighted. <em>May</em> 5. Got into Table Bay. <em>May</em> 6. Found letters left by the <em>Reformation</em> (which had departed for Bantam on April 14), the <em>Swan</em> (from Surat), and the <em>Advice</em> (from Bantam); the two latter had sailed for England on April 5. <em>May</em> 11. Their boat carried twenty natives to Penguin Island, 'there to resyde.' <em>May</em> 12. Sailed. <em>May</em> 30. Having had extremely bad weather, in which the ship had suffered severely and had been beaten back from the Cape, it was determined to bear up for Madagascar.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-029" href="#sec-06-fn-029" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-029" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> <em>June</em> 3. The wind changing, they stood for the Cape once more.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-030" href="#sec-06-fn-030" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-030" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> <em>June</em> 10. After enduring another storm, and finding the vessel very leaky, it was resolved to make for Madagascar again.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-031" href="#sec-06-fn-031" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-031" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> <em>June</em> 24. Abraham Aldington fell overboard, but was saved. <em>June</em> 28. Overtook the <em>William</em>, belonging to Courteen's Association and commanded by Edward Hall<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-032" href="#sec-06-fn-032" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-032" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup>; she was bound for India, and had lost company with her two consorts (a ship and a pinnace). <em>July</em> 3. Anchored in St. Augustine's Bay, and found there the <em>London</em>, under Captain Wills, bound for Surat.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-033" href="#sec-06-fn-033" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-033" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup> The <em>William</em> came in the same evening. <em>July</em> 9. The <em>Talbot</em>, vice-admiral of the <em>William's</em> fleet, came in. <em>July</em> 16. Those two vessels departed for Johanna. <em>July</em> 17. The <em>London</em> followed them. <em>August</em> 21. The <em>Mary</em> sailed for England. <em>September</em> 14. They estimated that they had doubled the Cape. <em>October</em> 9. Thought they saw St. Helena, but, finding that they had shot past the island, they resumed the voyage.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-034" href="#sec-06-fn-034" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-034" role="doc-noteref">6</a></sup> <em>October</em> 19. Crossed the Line.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-176"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="176" data-folio="122" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n175_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 122">[p. 122]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>November 7.</em> Joseph<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-035" href="#sec-06-fn-035" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-035" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, an Italian passenger, was put in irons for wounding Emanuel Fonseca, a Portuguese. <em>November 12.</em> The ‘coatch’ over the quarter deck was dismantled.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-036" href="#sec-06-fn-036" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-036" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> <em>November 26.</em> The Italian was released, on giving sureties. <em>November 29.</em> Sighted some of the Azores. <em>December 11.</em> Saw Scilly. <em>December 14.</em> Passed the Start. <em>December 16.</em> Anchored in the Downs. <em>December 21.</em> Methwold ‘went away for London’. <em>December 26, 27.</em> A severe storm. <em>December 29.</em> Sailed. <em>December 30.</em> Anchored in the Gore. 1640, <em>January 11.</em> Reached Erith. <em>January 13.</em> Paid off some of their sailors. (<em>Damaged.</em> 81 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">President Fremlen and Messrs. Breton and Robinson</span>, ABOARD THE <em>SWAN</em> AT SWALLY, TO THE COMPANY, JANUARY 15, 1639 (<em>O.C.</em> 1658).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Refer to their previous letter by the <em>Mary</em>, which sailed on the 4th. Since then they have been busy lading the <em>Swan</em>. Her cargo consists of the goods left over from the <em>Mary</em>, with some sugar candy, gum-lac, and sal-ammoniac added. The loss sustained by leakage in the <em>Michael</em> proves to be less than was expected. William Gibson’s notebook has been found and is now forwarded. Guy Bath is sent home to be dealt with by the Company. John Wilson, purser of the <em>Swan</em>, having died during her voyage from Masulipatam, John Jeffreys, their steward at Surat, has been promoted to that office. Jacob Cottenshaw, who from being mate in the <em>Mary</em> was made master of the <em>Blessing</em>, is blamed for his failure to reach the Red Sea, which has caused great discontent among the Surat merchants; he is therefore detained a year ‘to confront any theire publique allegations or more private charges’. He will be sent with the first vessel available to Mokha, calling on the way at Aden to pick up the English there and any other passengers; this, they hope, will give satisfaction. Enclose a petition from John Leachland’s wife,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-037" href="#sec-06-fn-037" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-037" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> and recommend it to the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-177" data-page="177" data-folio="123" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n176_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 123">[p. 123]</button> favourable consideration of the Company. Robert Amberson is sent home in this ship. He came out with Mr. Wilford, and on the death of the latter laid claim to his estate; but this was refused, partly because of his incapability and partly because no warrant could be found for his claim in Wilford's papers. He came back to Surat in the <em>Blessing</em>, and has since spent his time mostly in Ahmadābād and Cambay. The question what he shall pay for his diet on shore and on board, as also for his passage, is referred to the Company. A poor Walloon, named Glauda Godfrey, who came from Persia in the <em>Francis</em>, has been granted a passage in the <em>Swan</em> for charity's sake; also a Frenchman from Agra, who was recommended by the Jesuit Rector and by Mr. Bornford. Commendations of Thomas Steevens [master of the <em>Swan</em>]. Error in the invoice of the <em>Mary</em>. Stowage of the ginger. Room cannot be found for all the cotton yarn. Have already mentioned 'our new come Governor, Mier Moza, alias Mazel Mulck, who continues unto us all curteous and respective usage. Since the Presidents coming upp to Suratt, even the day after, arrived from Don Bras de Castro, Capt. Mor da Norte, together with the Capt[ain] and inhabitants of Damaon (where the caphila bound to Cambaiett hath bine deteyned about a months space, because of this Princes forces, which ruines and ransackes the townes belonging to the Portugalls) two qualified persons of that nation, sent to congratulate Mier Mozaes entrance to his government, to deliver severall letters from the Viceroy, the Capt. Mor, and townesmen of Damaon, and to treat of and reconcile, if may bee, those differences and troubles; untill the effecting wherof those letters and themselves declared the caphila [fleet: <em>see</em> p. 1] should not proceede to Cambaia, nor would they grant passes as accustomed to any shipp or shipps trading from any this Kings ports. This newes was most unwellcome to Mier Moza, who having ingaged himself to make good unto this King annually 200,000 m[ahmūdī]s [<em>cf.</em> p. 110] more then Messiah Zamon paid for this and Baroach customes, it imported him to indeavor the Portugalls sattisfaction by the best meanes hee could. And therfore [he] first entertaines these Portugalls respectively, promising mountaines (nor indeed can hee promise more then it <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-178" data-page="178" data-folio="124" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n177_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 124">[p. 124]</button> imports him to performe) and, sending for the President, intreats him, as well to assist him in this treaty as to make those Portugall letters speake Persian, that soe hee might the more punctually reply to them. This businesse was controverted three or four dayes; wherat hee would allwaies have your President present, the better to perswade the Portugalls to promise the dispeede of the cafila, and passes to all such as were desirous of them. Mier Moza on his part ingaged himself by most serious protestations to travayle himself in person to the Prince and there to negotiate and conclude a peace betwixt the Prince and [the] inhabitants of Damaon, soe as that they should not bee inforced to pay more to him then formerly they had bine accustomed to make good to the Rajah of Ramnuggur, or Rei da Sarceta<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-038" href="#sec-06-fn-038" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-038" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, as the Portugalls entitle him. Thus farr they had proceeded when by Messiah Zamon his procuration (having heard of Mier Mozaes coming and his owne remoovall from the government of Suratt) arrived the Princes letter writt to the Dutch Generall at Battavia, which Messiah Zamon, accompanied by the Dutch heere resident, with a great deale of ceremony went foorth of towne to receave; and which Mier Moza, after the Dutches bringing it home to theire house, sent for; when your President, beeing present, retired with Mier Moza into a withdrawing roome and heard the contents therof, which summarily is thus expressed: that in reguard the Prince (speaking of himself) had resided in and about Dowlettabaud neare three yeares, and that the Portugalls had not in all that time visitted him (which is to say, presented him with somewhat of vallue) hee had sent his servant Mier Moraad [Mīr Murād], attended with 5,000 horse and as many foote, to chastiz them. But having since understood that, although hee tooke theire country and citty of Damaon from them, yet themselves and theire goods might by sea escape his seizure; and having also beene informed that the Generall used anually to send 10 or 12 great and small shippes to Goa, hee now desired him to order the ensuing yeare such a nomber of shipps and friggotts to ride before Damon during the navigable monzoone that soe his owne people by land and the Dutch by sea might doe theire uttermost not only to reduce <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-179" data-page="179" data-folio="125" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n178_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 125">[p. 125]</button> them under his government but to captivate and seize theire persons and goods; for which service hee would gratify the Dutch with 200,000 rup[ee]s in ready money, the quarter part of the provenue of the whole country, and customes of all the goods they should land theare and export thence.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-039" href="#sec-06-fn-039" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-039" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> This discourse was as unwellcome as unpleasing not onely to Mier Moza but to the merchants of Suratt, who are now busily imploying their witts to divert this Princes intentions and to procure peace between him and the Portugalls; which doth soe much import them as that they will leave no meanes unattempted that but in appearance conduceth to its procurement. Nor will the Prince (considering the citties strength, conveniency of scituation, and unpassablenesse of the barr) soe easily and suddenly reduce it under his subjeccion as imagined. And yet, if by theire joynt endeavors it should soe come to passe, and that the Prince complies as fully as hee fairely promiseth the Dutch, nothing more advantagious on this coast could ever befall them; for theare and thence, as well as to and from Suratt, may they land and dispeed theire goods to theire inland factoryes, and no lesse comodiously imbarque and dispatch whatever they intend by sea to Battavia or elsewheare. Suratt [and] Cambayett will most suffer therby; whilest the trading vessells belonging to those portes will at all assaies bee subject to the assaults of the justly greived Portugalls. Litle advantage or praejudice will redound unto Your Worships affaires therby. Wee may chance to bee better used, least in our leaving Suratt the cheifest part of trade should with us cease to bee theare continued; as doubtlesse that conveniency which they have by our carrying theire goods for fraught occationeth, especially when theire enmity with the Portugall shall (as allready it doth) terrify them from exporting theire goods on these country vessells. They find allready to theire cares and costs the praejudice. The stopping of that land passage by Candahar having brought the merchants with theire goods to seeke passage by sea, heere also they find this without much hazard unpassable but by ours and the Dutch meanes, which neither is not procurable since your <em>Discovery</em> and theire <em>Busses ['sHertogenbosch's]</em> departure for Persia; insomuch that never to any mans remembrance were soe many merchants and goods present at any <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-180" data-page="180" data-folio="126" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n179_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 126">[p. 126]</button> one time in Suratt, all awayting transportation by yours or the Dutches shipping.' The <em>Diamond</em> has just arrived. She reached Gombroon on December 4 and, after a fortnight's stay, sailed again with 1,025 tūmāns in abbāsīs, five horses, and 154 bales of rūnās, besides freight goods. Of the goods carried thither by the <em>Francis</em>, the butter and oil (which were in bad condition) produced little or no profit, the tobacco was sold for 9 lārīs<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-040" href="#sec-06-fn-040" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-040" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> per maund, and the cloth yielded a gain of about 30 or 40 per cent. The cloves sent in the <em>Diamond</em> were sold for 101 lārīs per maund, which is a higher price than they would have fetched here. Three days before that vessel left Gombroon, the <em>Thomas</em> arrived there after a tedious voyage. She was expected to sail for Surat with freight goods early in the present month; and they propose then to send her once more to Persia, either direct or by way of Masulipatam. The pinnace <em>Francis</em> is about to be dispatched to Gombroon with freight goods and passengers and a cargo of cardamoms, sugar, indigo, &amp;c., on the Company's account. Edward Abbot and George Tash are sent to sell the goods and bring back the proceeds. Enclose a copy of the receipt given by Masīh-uz-Zamān for the 3,450 rials of eight repaid to him. <em>PS.</em>—Some private trade goods of John Wilson are on the <em>Swan</em>. The Frenchman to whom a passage was granted has not yet embarked, though the ship is ready to sail. (11 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">List of Packet of the</span> <em>SWAN</em>, <span class="sc">January</span> 15, 1639 (<em>O.C.</em> 1657).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Letters, invoices, bill of lading, inventory of the estate of John Cape, and 'two petitions from Manna Lichland [<em>see</em> p. 122], to the Honorable Company, &amp;c.' (1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2">[<span class="sc">William Methwold and others</span>], <span class="sc">aboard the</span> <em>MARY</em> <span class="sc">at</span> [<span class="sc">Cannanore</span>], <span class="sc">to</span> [<span class="sc">Captain Weddell there</span>], <span class="sc">January</span> 25, 1639 (<em>O.C.</em> 1659).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Reached Goa on January 10; and, whilst negotiating their business at that place, heard of the loss of the <em>Comfort</em> and the captivity of her crew. Thereupon they obtained letters of credit, directed to the Portuguese authorities at Cannanore, and have <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-181" data-page="181" data-folio="127" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n180_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 127">[p. 127]</button> now come hither to arrange for steps to ,be taken to ransom the prisoners.  Intend to sail for England immediately; and would be glad to take charge of any letters to be conveyed thither. (<em>Copy.</em>  ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">[<span class="sc">William Methwold and others, at Cannanore</span>] <span class="sc">to</span> [<span class="sc">Walter</span>] <span class="sc">Clark</span>, &amp;<span class="sc">c.</span>, <span class="sc">January</span> 25, 1639 (<em>Ibid.</em>).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Heard at Goa that an English ship had been taken or burnt by the Malabars, and her crew imprisoned; and a few days later a Portuguese brought news which confirmed them in their fears that it was the <em>Comfort</em> that had suffered this disaster.  Their informant computed the number of captives to be 18 or 20, including the master and purser; and he reported that the Malabars lost more than a thousand of their number before they could overpower the English.  Having ascertained that Cannanore was the best place from which to negotiate the ransom of the prisoners, they procured letters to the authorities of this place, desiring their assistance and empowering them to spend the necessary money, which will be repaid at Goa.  These letters Methwold and the rest are now sending ashore, together with the present note, and are about to proceed on their voyage, the time of the year and other considerations making it impossible for the ship to stay longer on this coast.  Recommend them to wait in patience till the Portuguese can negotiate their release, after which they should proceed to Goa and thence to Surat.  (<em>Copy.</em>  1½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">[<span class="sc">The Same</span>] <span class="sc">to</span> [<span class="sc">Captain Weddell, at Cannanore</span>], <span class="sc">January</span> 25, 1639 (<em>Ibid.</em>).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have now understood from Mr. Clark and his purser of Weddell's ‘noble favours’ in redeeming the captives.  Offer full satisfaction, either here (from moneys to be obtained on the letters of credit they have brought) or by bills of exchange on Goa, Surat, or elsewhere, as Weddell may please.  Beg that he will permit the released men to proceed in the <em>Planter</em> to Bhatkal, and then ‘they will take care of the rest’.  (<em>Copy.</em>  ½ <em>p.  Received, with the two preceding documents, from the Discovery, July</em> 1, 1640.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-182"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="182" data-folio="128" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n181_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 128">[p. 128]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">William Minors</span>, aboard the <em>Discovery</em> in Gombroon <span class="sc">Road, to the Company, January</span> 26, 1639 (<em>O.C.</em> 1660).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote from Surat by the <em>Mary</em> and <em>Swan</em>, giving an account of their voyage. They reached Cape De Verd on April 21, Cape ‘Bone Speranza’ on July 1, St. Augustine’s Bay on July 23, Johanna on August 6, and Surat on September 23. The Governor would not allow the President to repair on board; so Messrs. Fremlen and Breton came instead. Complains of having been forced to supply stores to other ships to the ‘disfurnishinge’ of his own. While at Swally he assisted to careen and repair various vessels. Left on December 22 with a freight amounting to 20,611½ mahmūdīs, and reached this port on January 14. The <em>Convoy</em> started with them, but lost company on the way and did not arrive till eight days after they did. Two days later came in a great Dutch ship, the ’<em>sHertogenbosch</em>, which had left Swally a day before the <em>Discovery</em>. Found here the <em>Thomas</em>, which had been eleven months on her voyage from Masulipatam. She was in great want, and they have spared her some stores, of which he sends a list. Three factors came from Surat in the <em>Discovery</em>: Edward Abbot, to dispose of the cargo, William Hall, to attend to customhouse affairs, and Thomas Wheeler, to assist generally. Has orders to take back to Surat Francis Honywood, who was formerly in charge here, but is now superseded by Hall. Thinks the change will be detrimental to the Company’s interests, for Honywood is much and deservedly respected by the Persians. Two days ago a couple of Dutch ships arrived from Batavia with spices; these ‘come well for them, to helpe satisfie their great ingagements, which heere and at Suratt is reported to amount unto two hundred thousand pounds starling’. His orders are not to stay more than twelve days, but the weather at present prevents his departure. He has a freight for Surat which will produce 700<em>l.</em> or 800<em>l.</em> Part of this has been transferred from the <em>Thomas</em>, which is now under orders to carry a Persian ambassador to Masulipatam. (2½ <em>pp. Received February</em> 22, 1640.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-183"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="183" data-folio="129" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n182_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 129">[p. 129]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Captain Weddell and John Mountney, aboard the <em>Dragon</em></span> [<span class="sc">at Cannanore</span>], <span class="sc">to</span> [<span class="sc">William Methwold</span>], <span class="sc">January</span> 26, 1639 (<em>O.C.</em> 1661).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Have received his letter by Mr. Clark. The amount paid as ransom was 2,200 rials of eight, and they would be glad to receive in satisfaction two bills on the East India Company, one for 400<em>l.</em>, payable to William Courteen and Company, and the other for 480<em>l.</em>, payable to John Weddell, Nathaniel Mountney, and Company,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-041" href="#sec-06-fn-041" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-041" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> the rial of eight being calculated at 8<em>s.</em> For the further sums spent in the relief of the captives, Mr. Clark and the purser will give their private bills. Will be ready to give passages to any of the Company's servants to Kārwār or Bhatkal, on receiving a list of their names. Complain that a sailor of their fleet, named John Wolvin, is on board the <em>Mary</em>, though the boatswain of the latter denies this; request his surrender. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">The Same to</span> [<span class="sc">the Same</span>], <span class="sc">January</span> 26, 1639 (<em>Ibid.</em>).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">John Mountney has heard that Methwold has sent ashore to ask the Portuguese to provide the 2,200 rials of eight, but that the latter, though willing, are unable to raise the money. Desire therefore that Methwold would think of some other method of repayment. (<em>Copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Captain Weddell, Nathaniel Mountney, and John Mountney, aboard the <em>Dragon</em></span> [<span class="sc">at Cannanore</span>], <span class="sc">to</span> [<span class="sc">William Methwold</span>], <span class="sc">January</span> 27, 1639 (<em>Ibid.</em>).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Express surprise at his change of attitude regarding the payment of the ransom. Bills on Surat or Goa would be useless to them; but they are willing to accept an undertaking that the money shall be paid at Kārwār or Bhatkal by March 15 next, with a proviso that in case of failure Methwold or the Company shall be responsible. If these terms be refused, they must carry the redeemed captives home, to obtain satisfaction there. If Methwold will not surrender the sailor he detains, he must take the responsibility. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p. Received, with the two preceding documents, by the</em> Discovery, <em>July</em> 1, 1640.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-184"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="184" data-folio="130" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n183_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 130">[p. 130]</button></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Captain Weddell, aboard the <em>Dragon</em> off Cannanore, to [President Fremlen at Surat], January 29, 1639</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-042" href="#sec-06-fn-042" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-042" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> (<em>O.C.</em> 1662).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Remembering the many favours received from him some years past, he sends by the bearer, Walter Clark, the account of their voyage asked for by his correspondent in a letter from Surat addressed to Mountney at Masulipatam. They left the Downs on April 14, 1636, a few hours after Captain Slade had set sail in the <em>Mary</em>. Narrative of the voyage. At Johanna heard from the <em>Mary</em> the death of 'your uncle, my good frend'.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-043" href="#sec-06-fn-043" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-043" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> Regrets much that the two ships 'came not together' earlier in the voyage. Reached Goa on October 6, and delivered His Majesty's letters and presents to the Viceroy. They were promised a ship's lading of pepper; but on the arrival of the 'caphilo' from Ceylon and the Malabar coast (January 12), they were told that it had been a dry year and there was no pepper to be had, 'which were nothinge but lies.' So they hastened to get in their money, and on January 17 departed and went to Bhatkal. There they obtained a concession from the King of 'Molinar' [<em>see</em> p. 5], and laded the <em>Planter</em> with pepper. It was hoped she would have got home that year; 'but they never putt for it, but went to St. Lawrence to feed on fresh beefes.' Next Weddell proceeded to Achin, where he was favourably received by the King and a factory was established. Reached Malacca on May 25, and Macao towards the end of June. Events there. Failure of their attempt to open up trade at Canton. Left Macao again on December 29. They were stopped by the Dutch in the Straits of Malacca, but an amicable agreement was reached, and the Hollanders showed them 'a way through the newe streights, which is three miles broad the narrowest place'. Anchored at Malacca on January 16 [1638], and departed three days later for Achin, where they arrived February 3. Left there the <em>Sun</em> to be laden for England. Weddell sailed on February 13 for India, and reached Cochin on March 9, having passed the <em>Jonas</em> on the way.</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-185"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="185" data-folio="131" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n184_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 131">[p. 131]</button></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Six Dutch ships belonging to the fleet blockading Goa came in on April 20, and their commander wanted to search the English vessels for Portuguese and their goods. ‘I told him, if he would breake open the Kinges house, he might; but that old foole, the Comaunder, tooke egges for his money,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-044" href="#sec-06-fn-044" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-044" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> and soe wee parted.’ Want of the ground tackle lost in China forced them to search for supplies at Masulipatam. ‘But our invitement thither was for their owne ends, and wee lent the Agent, Clarke, 1000 pounds starling in rials of eight and were glad to take cloves for the same at one dollar per lb., or elce wee should have had nothinge. What people the Companie hath there, I shame to speake of; but if the place were well manadged, it might produce the Companie proffitt enough.’ With much ado they got off their men and goods and departed. Touched at Armagon on September 22, left again on October 1, and next day met with a storm that endangered both their ships. Fears for the safety of the <em>Providence</em>, under Mr. Gardner, as her ground tackle was bad. Reached Bhatkal on November 15, and found there the <em>Planter</em> from England. Came to this place on November 26. (10½ <em>pp. Received at Surat March</em> 27, 1639, <em>and in London July</em> 1, 1640.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Contract between the Vedor da Fazenda</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-045" href="#sec-06-fn-045" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-045" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> <span class="sc">at Goa and Messrs. Andrew Cogan and John Wylde</span>, February 12/22, 1639 (<em>O.C.</em> 1620).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The English factors undertake to provide one great ship and, if possible, a smaller one; failing the latter, they will furnish mariners to man such a vessel. Rates of freight for gunpowder and shot. The English are to provide rice and lead, to be sold at Malacca at fixed rates, and to be paid for by bills on Macao. Ordnance, &amp;c., to be brought from thence. The English may employ 3,000 rials at Macao for their own account, paying the customs of Malacca for the same. At Goa customs will only be required on goods landed there. Only the merchants will be allowed on shore at Macao. No passengers to be carried save those appointed. Payment for <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-186" data-page="186" data-folio="132" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n185_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 132">[p. 132]</button> freight goods will be made within fifteen days of their being landed. (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <sup>3</sup>/<sub>4</sub> <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">THOMAS MERRY AND THOMAS ADLER [AT ISPAHĀN] TO THE PRESIDENT AND COUNCIL AT SURAT, FEBRUARY 28, 1639 (<em>O.C.</em> 1665).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote last on December 26 ; now answer the Surat letter of the 20th idem. Complain of being addressed therein in terms of contempt and derision ; without disputing the supremacy of the President, 'wee howld the ordring of the affaires of this kingdom belonges unto us,' at all events until the Company send positive directions to the contrary. Assert that they have already put an end to former disorders. As regards Chapman's action in charging them from Gombroon with 2,000 tūmāns, they still maintain that it was unadvised and without authority, and that they were justified in refusing to accept this liability. Here they have not the means to pay, nor do they intend to meddle at all in the matter ; so the President and Council must themselves return to the Dutch with interest the money they received on this account. Controvert the interpretation put upon the Company's instructions regarding a new contract with the Shāh, &amp;c. ; and answer in detail accusations of tautology and forgetfulness in their former letter. Deny that their tin could have been sold for money or bartered for silk at a reasonable rate. They trust that their proceedings will be approved at home, even although they cause displeasure at Surat. (<em>Copy.</em> 7 <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3">THE TRIAL OF JOHN PERKINS, MARCH 12, 1639<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-046" href="#sec-06-fn-046" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-046" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> (<em>O.C.</em> 1667).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The Court, composed of the President and Council of Surat, together with Captain Minors, being duly assembled, the prisoner was brought to the bar and charged with having caused the death of James Congden. He entered a plea of not guilty, whereupon a jury of twelve was empannelled. Thomas Cogan gave evidence that he and Perkins went on February 21 to the English garden, carrying with them a gun and a half-pint bottle of arrack. Cogan fired twice at some birds and then returned the gun to Perkins, who loaded it again. 'After which they stript themselves to go wash ; <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-187" data-page="187" data-folio="133" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n186_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 133">[p. 133]</button> and, as they were ready to go into the tank<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-047" href="#sec-06-fn-047" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-047" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, James Congden came to them. So they washed themselves all three together about the space of halfe an hower; and while they were in the water drank up the forementioned arrack between them. Then they went together out of the water, and this deponent and John Perkins presently clothed themselves, whilest James Congden sate still and made no hast to put on his clothes. So they being first ready went to walke in the garden. And John Perkins having his birding peece in his hands espied a bird and bent the cock of his peece, but whether quite up or but halfe way the deponent is not certaine; but the bird flying away before the peece was presented, the said John Perkins shott not off, but kept the peece still in his hands with the cock still bent. And at that instant meeting John Perkins his servant with the bottle, which they sent him to fill the second time with arrack, they both went back with him towards the tanck, where James Congden was sitting dressing of himselfe. And being come within 10 or 12 paces of him, the said John Perkins, holding his peece with the muzle of it towards James Congden, without setting it towards his shoulder or seeming to take any aime through the sight, called out in these words: "Beware, James"; at which James Congden looking up, the peece went of and shott him upon the chinn and into the throat; wherupon he fell downe and died immediately.' Next, the prisoner, being asked what he could say for himself, gave a similar account of the incident. The jury retired, but, after debating some time, returned and examined two more witnesses, who deposed that they knew of no ill-feeling or malice between the prisoner and the deceased. After further deliberation, the jury acquitted Perkins both of murder and manslaughter, 'finding it to be but chance medly.' The gun was examined in court and found to be so constructed that the cock would go down when only half bent. On hearing the verdict, the President declared the prisoner acquitted, and ordered him to be released.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-048" href="#sec-06-fn-048" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-048" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> (3¾ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-188"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="188" data-folio="134" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n187_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 134">[p. 134]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2">HENRY BORNFORD'S ACCOUNT OF HIS JOURNEY FROM AGRA TO TATTA [? MARCH, 1639] (<em>O.C.</em> 1669).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">'From Agra to Lahoare are counted 300 course<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-049" href="#sec-06-fn-049" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-049" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, 22 mounzells [<em>see</em> p. 2] or ordinary dayes journies. Places of noate bettweene them are divers. First Dillie [Delhi], some 80 course from Agra, formerly the seate and title of these kings, now less famous, and little frequented unless by Armenian and Persian merchants; the comodity that invites them thither being only chints, which are heere made in good quantities, well cullored, in appearance little inferiour to those of Mesulapatam; different sorts and goodnes, and soe diversly prized. Thence to Paniputt [Pānīpat] are 42 course: an indifferent large towne, in which is made much white cloth of the lengthes and breadths of those of Semiano<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-050" href="#sec-06-fn-050" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-050" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, 11 covetts greate in length and <sup>3</sup>/<sub>4</sub> in breadth; sent from hence to Seiound [Sirhind] and Lahoare for sale. Thirtie course from this is Tanesa or Taneceere [Thānesar], which affoards store of conserves, as mangoes, hurly<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-051" href="#sec-06-fn-051" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-051" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>, [and] ginger; which latter was then to be bought at 5 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub> seere pucke [<em>see the 1618–21 volume</em>, p. 74] per rupie. This is likewise the place of making nossadur<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-052" href="#sec-06-fn-052" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-052" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup>, whose price was nowe 6 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub> ru[pees] per greate maen, and seldome or never that it passeth 7 rupies. The third munzell from hence is Seroun or Senound [Sirhind], greatlie frequented by the abovenamed merchants for chints, redd sealaes, comodities of that place; sugar and sugar candied, the former made at Serwerperpore<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-053" href="#sec-06-fn-053" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-053" role="doc-noteref">5</a></sup>, 40 course wide of Seroun, and heere prized at 5 <sup>1</sup>/<sub>4</sub> ru[pees] per maen; not equalling that of Agra, there being butt little soe well coulered. The later is brought from Muhum [Maham, in Rohtak District], standing wide bettweene Dillie and this place 50 course, and was prized heere at 12 ru[pees] per maen. Hither from Semiano, 25 course <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-189" data-page="189" data-folio="135" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n188_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 135">[p. 135]</button> distant, is brought much of the clothing. All which comodities are by the Persian and Armenian transported to Spahan and other parts thereabouts, by the way of Candahar. From this towne is 7 munzells to Lahoare, the prime citty of traffick in India; all comodities of the adjacent places being brought hither, and are bought by the Wousbecks [Uzbegs] or Tartarrs, and soe transported by Cabull into those parts, and by those of Casmeere, as alsoe by the abovenamed merchants. The prizes of sugar and other comodities at present being there were as followeth, vizt. sugar, the best (being white and full graine, not much less in goodnes then that at Agra) was at 7 rup[ees] per maen packa, and the second sort at 5¾ and 6; sugar-candy, 11 rup[ees], which is made in this place in greate quantities; these are usually transported by the Tuttah merchants downe the river and thence exported to Muscatt, Congo, Bursora, &amp;c.; nosshader, 10 rup[ees]; all sorts of clothing, as suffocannes<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-054" href="#sec-06-fn-054" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-054" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, cantars<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-055" href="#sec-06-fn-055" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-055" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, chints, salooes, according to theire goodnes; the former from above 40 to 60 rup[ee]s per cor[ge]; cantaurs at 30 ru[pees] per cor[ge]; chints from 30 to 36 and 37 per cor[ge]; salloos at 32 per cor[ge]. Hither is likewise brought the greater quantity of the Goria<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-056" href="#sec-06-fn-056" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-056" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> indicoe, heere well esteemed and worth 80 ru[pees] the greate maen. Freight or cartage of goods from the severall places to Lahoare &amp;c. is as followes, vizt. from Agra (if the lascar<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-057" href="#sec-06-fn-057" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-057" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup> be not that way) usually not above 2 rup[ee]s per maen; to Lahoare from Serwerpore (whence the greatest part [of] the sugarr comes to ditto place) 1 rup[ee] for a maen; from thence to Maltan, 2½ ru[pees]; from Seround to Lahoare is ¾ ru[pee] per maen; thence to Multan, 1½ and 1¾; from Lahoare to Multan 46 s[ee]re per rup[ee]; from Agra to Multan 2½ ru[pees] per maen [and] 35 dayes passage. From Lahoare to Tutta the usuall transport of goods is downe the river in flatt bottom boates of a thousand and 2,000 maens; first by Multan, 150 course; 11 dayes jorney by land, and in soe much tyme we accomplished it by water. Heere is paid custome of all goods that either goe for Candahar or else downe the river to Sinda, at 2½ per <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-190" data-page="190" data-folio="136" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n189_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 136">[p. 136]</button> cent., besides some other charges at the Gaut [<em>ghāt</em>] or passage, which will amount [to] ¼ per cent. more. Freight from hence to Tuttah is ¾ rup[ee] per maen; but the best way is to buy a boate, which heere are cheape in respect of Lahoare, one at 2,000 maens burthen not costing more then 250 ru[pees] or thereabouts; which may likewise be sold in Tuttah, if not to proffitt, yett to the price it cost. The roderiee [<em>rāhdārī: see</em> p. 13] of the waye for all boates above 500 maens to 2,000 will not fall much under 1000 rup[ees], besides payment of marriners and soldiers for its guard, which cannot be less then 10 or 12 of the former and 20 of the latter. The goods for payment [of] customes in this place are vallued according to the bazar rate, though for 1000 ru[pees] of goods sold together noe mann will give soe much by 40 per cent. The valew of Byana indicoe was at 85 ru[pees] the great maen; sugar candy, 15 ru[pees] per maen; sugar, 10 ru[pees]; and other comodities of cloth according to theire different sorts, the rates continually varying. In Multan are made chints course, and white cloth; the former in some quantity, the latter not much. Hither is likewise sometimes brought course sugar and sugarcandy, sold to the Tuttah merchants. From Multan the next place of noate is Durbelah<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-058" href="#sec-06-fn-058" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-058" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, where is made of those narrowe joories [<em>see the last volume</em>, p. 133] or baftaes, of lengths of those in Narsrapore [Nasarpur: <em>see the last volume</em>, p. 128], 16 and 17 greate covets, but not soe broad, are heere at 17 and 18 ru[pees] per cor[ge]. From hence is Bucker [Bukkur], 200 course from Multan. Heere is large quantitie of oyle and butter sold at 7½ s[ee]r per rup[ee], and is passed downe the river to Tuttah. Betweene Bucker and Sewan [Sehwān: <em>see the last volume</em>, p. 129] is 150 course; where is made quantities of narrowe baftaes bearing the name of Sewan joories; bought heere for ¾ to 1¼ rup[ees] per peece, of finer making then those of Nasrapoor [and] 2 covetts shorter in length. Neare this towne, in Bubecka<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-059" href="#sec-06-fn-059" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-059" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> and Sun<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-060" href="#sec-06-fn-060" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-060" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>, is made the indicoe transported for sale to Tuttah; the quantitye by nearest inquirie was not more then 1000 greate maens, and there will cost newe and wett 21 and 22 rup[ee]s per <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-191" data-page="191" data-folio="137" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n190_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 137">[p. 137]</button> maen of 42 pice per sear. Charges of custome: to the Governor is 4 ru[pees] per maen; to the towne 1 rup[ee]; and freight to Tuttah ½ a rup[ee]. Indico, not good in appearance, indifferentlie esteemed, and is by the Tuttah merchants transported to Barsora [Basrā], where it readily vends to good proffitt; the quantity noe doubt may be increased, if the quallity cannot be bettered. Bettweene Swean [<em>sic</em>] and Sur [? Sun] till Halicandy<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-061" href="#sec-06-fn-061" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-061" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> (40 course) is alwayes some daunger; but was not nowe passable without a strong guard, the Samidas<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-062" href="#sec-06-fn-062" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-062" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> (whose country lieth alongst the other side [of] the river) being at variance with that Governor. In the midway from hence to Tuttah is Nasrapore, a place which hath formerly yeilded greater quantities of those narrow baftaes when the Portingalls trade flourished, being by them bought. Now if a settled residence be continued in that place, and likewise monies bee imprested unto those weavors, in a short tyme it will produce double the quantitie and cheaper; those being bought for 18 and 19 ru[pees] per cor[ge] in Nasrapore which in Tuttah are sold at 20 and 23. The charges are not greate in soe small a distance.’ Tatta and the places adjoining are already well known, owing to Fremlen’s residence there. ‘Comodities wants not in all the premencioned places to the producing a large trade. For transport of goods from Lahoare &amp;c. downe the river, the best tyme is to part thence in March, soe if possible to arrive before the raynes enter, when, the winds being violent, a laden boate cannot without much danger continue the voyage, and so it comes to be a months more expence of tyme in wayting opportunities of weather; but the soonest that in the best tyme a laden boate cann pass is three months, being extraordinaryly tarded in payment of the severall choukies [toll-stations: <em>chaukī</em>] on the way. Whether it be the better transport for Agra goods, the sevirall charges calculated, will soone be decided. Nowe is the better way to it, in reguard Asaph Caun hath remitted neere the halfe of those extraordinary customs in Bandar Larree. From Multan the river is navigable at all times; but from Lahoare in the begining [of] March till the coole tyme enter in October. The way by Jessamoore [Jaisalmer] is much nearer, being not above 35 daies <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-192" data-page="192" data-folio="138" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n191_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 138">[p. 138]</button> or 40 at the best time, and only [difficult ?] of its passing presently after the raynes. Customes there are none of any consequence but in Jessameere; and there the goods are vallued and pay (according as by informacion) 12 per cent.; but this perhapps may be remitted and quallified by fore agreement with the Rajah. The portage being on cammells, the hire of each wilbe 22 ru[pees] or thereabouts. The sevirall comodities of each place in this passage that are to be bought is summarily declared. For the vend of ought that comes from any forraigne clymate, as the country wants little, so gives noe price for them in any quantitie, unless in the Kings laskar, that part of this countries commerce continually attending him.' (<em>Copy</em>. 2¾ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Captain Weddell to Antonio de Moura de Brito, Captain of Cochin</span> [? <span class="sc">March</span>, 1639] (<em>O.C.</em> 1663).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-063" href="#sec-06-fn-063" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-063" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Regrets that he is prevented by indisposition from waiting on the Captain in person. Desires to warn him about the two East India Company's ships at Goa, as he understands that there is some idea of freighting them for China. The larger of the two, called the <em>Blessing</em>, arrived from England five years ago, and is now considered too old for the voyage back to that country. Knowing well the weather conditions of the China seas, Weddell is convinced that such a vessel would be unfit for the venture; and moreover, she is not of sufficient force to secure her lading against seizure by the Dutch, who did not hesitate to stop Weddell's ships, though they were new and well manned. Feels bound to give this warning, lest the King of England should be held responsible for any loss that may accrue. (<em>Portuguese.</em> ¾ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Walter Clark at Surat to the President and Council at Bantam, April</span> 1, 1639 (<em>O.C.</em> 1671).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">They left Masulipatam on October 26, and made the best of their way towards Swally. By November 16 they had reached the latitude of 11° 20′, where they were chased from early morning by nine frigates. By eight o'clock the Malabars were within range, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-193" data-page="193" data-folio="139" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n192_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 139">[p. 139]</button> and then for some time they kept their distance; but finding at noon that there was a flat calm, ‘insomuch they perceived our shippe could not worke any way with her sayles, they handed theyr sayles and immediatly rew all together on board us and lashed fast, notwithstanding wee placed every shott into them and spoyled many of theyr people. Being lashed on board, they entred theyr men in abundance, the which wee used all meanes possible to cleare; but finding them so resolutly bent, and still encreasing so abundantly, I resolved to blow up our upper deck, and effected it with the losse of not one of our people, yet some hurt, and divers of theyrs (namely, the Mallabarrs) slayne and maimed. This seemed little or nothing to diminish or quell theyr courage; but wee still continued to defend [i.e. repel] the opposing enemy by murthering and wounding each other, they being so resolut that they would not step aside from the muzzell of our ordnance when wee fired uppon them but immediately, being fired, heave in whole bucketts of water; insomuch that in the conclusion wee were forced to betake ourselves unto the gundeck, uppon which wee had but two pieces of ordnance. They then cutting with axes the deck over our heads, and hearing the hideous noyse and cry of such a multitude, [we] thought how to contrive a way to send them all to theyr greate adorer, Belzebub, which was by firing all our powder at one blast, as many of us as were left alive leaping into the sea, yet intercepted (some) by those divelish helhou[nds]. Wee were at that present English 23 (being all wound[ed], foure excepted), blacks 4, and Javaes 4: slayne, English 5, Jav[aes] 3, and blacks 13. All which were then living they tooke into theyr friggotts and carried us on shoare about 20 houres af[ter]; where wee, the English, wanted all things whatsoever, irons, hunger, and cold only excepted. . . . During all this time of our encounter, which was from eight [in] the morning untill four in the afternoone, there was not mo[re] then three leagues distance from us a Dutch shippe, which cou[ld] not by any meanes assist us, in regard of its being calm; yet at six or seven in the evening in our lee came fayre by the shippe burning; and so she continued, the enemy not gaining ought that belonged to the Honourable Company, but was enforced to leave her, with the losse of more then 1,400 men.’ After two days’ imprisonment they received a letter from Weddell’s factors at Cannanore, inquiring <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-194" data-page="194" data-folio="140" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n193_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 140">[p. 140]</button> whether they were English or Dutch; ‘after which information they releived us with what wee desired, sending every six daies an English man unto us, named Henry Weygive, who formerly was fugitive from the Company and turned Moore.’ About five days after their imprisonment, the Governor told them that, unless they could speedily procure a ransom, they would be put to death; whereupon Clark wrote to the Cannanore factors, begging them to convey a letter from him to Surat, by way of Bhatkal, where Weddell was then riding. The latter at once sent to Surat, and himself wrote that he would be at Cannanore within twenty days and would then do his best to effect their release. At first the Governor demanded 3,500 rials of eight, but he was persuaded by some Portuguese prisoners to agree to accept 2,200; and when the appointed time was expired and there was no sign of Weddell, he put them aboard three of his frigates (being all he could now man) and sent them to Bhatkal. There, on January 4, they were made over to Weddell on payment of the amount agreed upon. On the arrival of Methwold at Cannanore, he used means to free them from Weddell, who would otherwise have taken them to Europe; and on February 13 they reached Goa, where they found the <em>Blessing</em> and <em>Michael</em> with Andrew Cogan and John Wylde. Clark and Wylde departed at once for Surat ‘uppon an almadia’ [country boat: <em>see the 1630–33 volume</em>, p. 101]. There they found the <em>Discovery</em> lading for Mokha. Clark has now been appointed master of the <em>Michael</em>. (<em>Copy.</em> 3½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">George Muschamp</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-064" href="#sec-06-fn-064" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-064" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, <span class="sc">aboard the</span> <em>REFORMATION</em> <span class="sc">near Table Bay, to the Company, April</span> 8, 1639 (<em>O.C.</em> 1672).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has just met the <em>Swan</em> (from Surat) and the <em>Advice</em> (from Bantam) leaving Table Bay. . . . Understands from those on board that there is a want of ships in the East. Has also heard of scandalous abuses, which he will do his best to [rectify. Urges the Company to send out a supply of honest and able men. The want of such, especially on the Coromandel Coast, has not only exhausted a large part of the Company’s estate, but has also led to a loss of reputation and the hazarding of their dearly bought privileges, owing to the <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-195" data-page="195" data-folio="141" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n194_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 141">[p. 141]</button> action of the factors in endeavouring to save Weddell from paying customs. Hopes that Cogan will reduce them to a better conformity; and promises his own assistance to that end. Thinks that a small ship should be sent direct to Masulipatam, to be there in May, 1640, and then proceed to Bantam. . . . (<sup>3</sup>/<sub>4</sub> <em>p. Seal.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Andrew Cogan at Goa to the Agent and Council at Masulipatam, May</span> 20, 1639 (<em>O.C.</em> 1674).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Left Surat on May 4, and reached this place a week later. Found here 'a Portugall nam'd John Pinto, sent hither by the Danes to make tender of their shipping for the releife of Malaca; which is entertained by the V[ice] R[oy], but on what condicions as yett [I] know not'.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-065" href="#sec-06-fn-065" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-065" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> This man promised to carry letters from Cogan to Masulipatam, but went away secretly without giving him notice, 'feareing I might advize of the contract.' Wrote them, however, on February 15 by another Portuguese, giving all the news, including the contract he had made with the Viceroy for sending two ships to China; 'which occasioned mee to leave heere 12,000 rials of eight to buy rice to be transported to Mallaca on the Companies account; but because twas late er wee began to treate (wherein the Viz King is to be blamed), the <em>Discovery</em> was laden for Mocha and reddy to sett saile er newes came to Surrat of what I had done, and (haveing noe shippe to receive her goods) she proceeded on her voiage; which the Portugalls haveing timely notiz of from the President at Surrat, fitted themselves time enough with four small vessells for the premencioned imployment, and in their way to China to land 600 candies of rice at Mallaca. Soe that it behooves us as soone as was possible to gett into our hands againe the aforesaid 12,000 rials of eight; which (and only it) occasion[ed] of takeing Goa in my way towards you. Had the <em>Diamond</em> arived or the <em>Francis</em> bine fitt for navigacion, I had come on one of them; on whome should have beene laden a cargazoon of cloth for Bantam, which is provided and redy at Surrat [but] now for want of shiping must there continue till August'; for the <em>Diamond</em> is believed to be wintering at 'Sinda' and the <em>Francis</em> must be trimmed ere she can put to sea. Grieves <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-196" data-page="196" data-folio="142" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n195_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 142">[p. 142]</button> to learn the lamentable state of affairs at Masulipatam, but hopes the Company will send them supplies shortly. The money already mentioned is to be paid to him on the 23rd instant. The <em>Thomas</em> left this port for Masulipatam on April 29; on her arrival she should be at once trimmed, in preparation for a voyage which he dares not mention now, for fear this letter should miscarry. Cogan hopes to start by the 25th, and will be accompanied by three factors, including Greenhill. On his way he will visit Golconda, where he would be glad to find a letter from Masulipatam. Proposes to bring Rogers with him from thence, unless there is good reason to the contrary. <em>PS. (May 24)</em>—Has been unable hitherto to send off a pattamar, owing to bad weather; and his business is now again put off for three days longer. On the 21st arrived from Surat a vessel of about 60 tons, called the <em>Prosperous</em>. In her were William Pitt and George Tash, with a cargo of rūnās for Rājāpur. By the unskilfulness of her master she was put by her port; but she is to sail thither with the first fair wind, and meanwhile Pitt starts overland for that place to-day. She left at Surat two Dutch ships from Persia, who were lading for Batavia. Their commander was named ‘Van Der Brooke<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-066" href="#sec-06-fn-066" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-066" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’. (<em>Signed copy.</em> 2½ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Richard Hudson and Thomas Peniston at Masulipatam to Andrew Cogan [at Golconda], June 11, 1639</span> (<em>O.C.</em> 1681).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The <em>Thomas</em> arrived on May 28, and will shortly be sent to Narsapur for repair. Have been unable to get any accounts from Rogers. They formerly thought that his stay in Golconda was voluntary, but it appears otherwise, and that he is not suffered to go out of the city without a guard. He can inform Cogan fully of their grievances. ‘If that hellhound the Serkale should bring you before His Majestie, we presume your complaints will never bee heard; therefore could wish rather that you would plead your cause at sea, for on land wee shall reape nothing but faire words.’ ‘As the newes goes heere, you may sudenly expect the Dutch with a very large present; therefore (if possible) could wish your departure er they arrive, for their large guifts and our emptie hands <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-197" data-page="197" data-folio="143" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n196_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 143">[p. 143]</button> shall procure the former respect and us disgrace; for, say what they will, there is noe Moore a freind longer then what will you give mee.' (<em>Copy.</em> 1 <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Richard Hudson at Masulipatam to Andrew Cogan [at Golconda], July</span> 16, 1639 (<em>O.C.</em> 1681).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">The <em>Thomas</em> was unable to get over the bar at Narsapur; she is now trying to get into the river, but, if she cannot do so by the 22nd, she will return to this place. The Dutch embassy to Golconda has been delayed by the death of a great elephant and two Persian horses; 'soe that I hope that they are staied for this yeare, which willbe litle for their ease, seing they have promised a vizett anie time these two yeares.' Forwards a letter received from Surat on the 13th. (<em>Copy.</em> ¾ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Andrew Cogan at Golconda to the Factors at Masulipatam, July</span> 22, 1639 (<em>O.C.</em> 1682).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-067" href="#sec-06-fn-067" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-067" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">After much difficulty he arrived on the 19th about five kos from Golconda, where Rogers met him and delivered the letter from Masulipatam of June 11. Would gladly have avoided entering the city and visiting the King, for he feared that this would only entail a great outlay for presents, without adequate return, and his journey had already been very chargeable. However, Rogers assured him that it would not be safe, or at least would be very prejudicial to their affairs, if he passed by the city without acquainting the 'Serkaile', as his approach was already known to the King. As for the grievances to be brought to the notice of the latter, he referred him to the paper lately received from Masulipatam. A present must, Rogers said, be given, for none had been offered for three years, and the last one had been refused as too small. After considering these matters, and finding that he could not otherwise get 'pulankeene men' for his journey, on the 20th Cogan came within two miles of the city and wrote a few lines to the 'Sirkaile', who replied that the King desired to see him, at the same time assuring him that 'wherin hee could, would be my servant in the behalfe of the Company'. Does <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-198" data-page="198" data-folio="144" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n197_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 144">[p. 144]</button> not credit these assurances, for he perceives by the Masulipatam letters that the Sar-i-Khail ‘hath beine our great enemy’; but evidently he cannot avoid visiting him and the King, ‘who will expect great presents, conformable to what hath bein formerly given, but their expectations wilbe frustrate, although something must be done.’ For details of his journey he refers to the bearer, but declares that the delay has been due to no fault of his. Notes the arrangements for repairing the <em>Thomas</em>. As the season is far spent and his arrival is uncertain (though he trusts it will not be later than August 10), he now acquaints them with his intentions in regard to her. In order to obtain the money already mentioned, he was in a manner forced to promise the Portuguese that she should be sent to Malacca with 1,000 candies of rice, for which they are to pay seven rials of eight per candy. Thinks that this should prove a very beneficial voyage, as the rice can be bought in the Bay; and moreover he knows of no better employment for the ship, which may then either take in freight for Goa or proceed to Pegu and there invest the money in copper and tin for the Coast. They should confer with the master about all this, but should warn him to keep the matter secret, lest the Dutch should hinder the voyage. Dispatches Henry Greenhill, ‘a discreet understanding man,’ to their assistance with 3,000 ‘St. Amees<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-068" href="#sec-06-fn-068" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-068" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’ for early investment for Europe. Has now with him 9,000 rials of eight in San Thomés; in addition to which he intends to draw on the Vedor at Goa for 3,000 more, and on Surat for 7,000 pagodas. This money, with what may be expected from England or Bantam, ought to clear the Coast from debt and provide a large cargo of goods. Will do his best to collect the debts outstanding here, and also to bring away Rogers. While at Goa he received from Alvaro de Souza de Castro 2,000 rials of eight, which he promised to convey to Achin for delivery to Francisco de Souza de Castro, ‘our espetiall good freind, who was sent imbassador from Goa and there by that treacherous King kept prizoner.’ Begs that if they hear of any junk bound for that port, they will send therein an Englishman on some plausible excuse to deliver this money; or perhaps the <em>Thomas</em> might call there on her way from Malacca. Rejoices to hear that they have lessened the Company’s expenses <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-199" data-page="199" data-folio="145" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n198_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 145">[p. 145]</button> by discharging a number of unnecessary servants; but he thinks they might also reduce their stable, for he cannot see how they can want ten or more horses for that factory. <em>PS.</em>—Begs for an account of what is due from ‘the Deber Meizatuck<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-069" href="#sec-06-fn-069" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-069" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’, that he may recover the amount. (<em>Copy.</em> 5 <em>pp. Received July</em> 31.)</p></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Andrew Cogan at Golconda to the Factors at Masulipatam, July</span> 30, 1639 (<em>O.C.</em> 1683).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Trusts that Greenhill has long ere this brought the news of his arrival at Golconda, where he has dispatched all his business as well as could be wished; ‘for the Kinge after a faire reception the 28th instant granted all I could desire. Twilbe needless to repeate the particulars, because am promisd the 14th this moone to have my dispatch, after which tyme hope within few dayes to be with you. In the meane time I bless God for the safe arrivall of our ship with you. The newes was told mee last eveninge by the Serkayle, my most loving freinde, of whome I shall say more hearafter, for surely, when I peruse your letters ettc., I cannot but even admire of his change; and because you shall not thinke what hee hath done for mee was in hopes of gaine, the present that I sent him (which was not much inferiour to the Kings) hee retorn’d mee againe (only reservd a piece of ambergreese which might weigh about a sare) with the proffession of more love and freindship then indeed I am able to express. From other of the nobillity I have receavd good respect and promise wherin they may theyle be my freind. Elche Begg yesterday came into the citty and was received by the Kinge with a greate deale of love. To him as yett I have not spoke conserninge what hee owes the Company, but intend to-morrow to send or write to him conserninge it.’ Inquires the name of the ship just arrived,<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-070" href="#sec-06-fn-070" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-070" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> with particulars of her cargo &amp;c., for communication to Surat. (<em>Signed copy.</em> ½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-200"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="200" data-folio="146" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n199_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 146">[p. 146]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">Thomas Ivy and Thomas Morris</span> [<span class="sc">at Masulipatam</span>] <span class="sc">to Andrew Cogan at Golconda, August</span> 1, 1639 (<em>O.C.</em> 1684).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Wrote last on July 29; and yesterday received his of the 22nd. Rejoice at the news of his arrival, and trust he will be able to recover the debts outstanding, including that of ‘Meire Mahmud Side’ of 1,900 pagodas, ‘trusted him by Mr. Joyce.’ ‘Likwise wee would desire you to procure of the Kinge, though it cost the Company some moneye (which wee leave to your discression), a firman for the procureing of what debts are oweing in these parts under his goverment, and that wee may have free leve to take or ceaze upon any mans goods that standeth ingadged unto our honnorable imployers; or else wee are like to have but bad justice for 10,000 pagodas that is oweing here and at Verasharoone and other places. Since our here arivall wee have found very good quarter and are not debarred of any priviledge formerly granted to Mr. Joyce, with many promises of our seemeing freind the Governour that wee shall have more freedome then ever wee had.’ ‘Your intended voyag concerning shipp <em>Thomas</em> for Mallacca and Goa is not further to be thought of, though questionless by you intended for the good of the Company; nether will our President of Bantam, to whom wee are subordinat, aprove of any such projecte to send the Companyes shipps into the lyons clawes, wee meeane with [<em>sic</em>] our competitours the Dutch, who hath with the Kinge of Acheen beeseiged Mallacca by land and sea, soe that there cannot a boate escape them; and they would desire noe better purchase [i. e. prize] then to meet with any of our shipps laden with provisions for the releife of their proclaimed enemyes, which they may by the law of nations make priz of. And what will not these insolent people atempt to the disparagment of our nation, that will not lett any one our shipps pass in the South Seas but will comand them to strick and will not lett them pass untill they have seene whether they have any Portugall goods aboard.’ Instance their interference with the <em>Providence, Eagle</em>, and <em>Expedition</em>. Moreover, the <em>Thomas</em> is urgently needed at Bantam, whither they intend to dispatch her as soon as she is trimmed. Greenhill has arrived with the 3,000 San Thomés. The 26,000 rials of eight which Cogan promises to bring will serve to discharge part of their debts, which, as the interest is <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-201" data-page="201" data-folio="147" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n200_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 147">[p. 147]</button> three per cent. per month, must be cleared off before any further investment can be thought of. As regards the 2,000 rials of eight to be transmitted to Achin, they propose to ask the President at Bantam to make the remittance by way of Jambi. Enclose the account of ‘Debere Meiza Tuck’. Yesterday Peniston and Winter<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-071" href="#sec-06-fn-071" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-071" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> were sent to Narsapur and Vīravāsaram. (<em>Copy.</em> 3¼ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Andrew Cogan, near Golconda, to Thomas Ivy and other Factors at Masulipatam, August</span> 9, 1639 (<em>O.C.</em> 1686).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Has received theirs of July 20 and August 1. Notes the arrival of the <em>Eagle</em> from Bantam, but censures their omission to inform him what cargo she brought. ‘Secondly, you tell me what power the President of Bantam hath conferr’d upon the Agent, by vertue of which (never remembring that clause in your comission which tells you [you] must subscribe to the Companies order) you lay one me your perimitorie comaunds; which Ile obey as is fitting, but good manners wold have taught you more modestie, knowing my imployment from the Company is noe way inferriour to any yours.’ Had already determined to dissolve this unprofitable factory and carry Rogers with him to account ‘in Bundar<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-072" href="#sec-06-fn-072" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-072" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>’. ‘To that end a three daies since have freed the howse and removed myselfe &amp;c. to a garden a mile without the towne; where I awaite my firmands from the King; the contents of which firmands are to recover all such monies as is due and hath bine wrongfully taken from the Company.’ Next, they ‘will and comaund’ him to send all his money forthwith to them to defray their necessary charges. Had they wanted it to pay their debts or to buy goods he might have complied; but he fears they would only spend it in unnecessary charges, and so it is better where it is. Besides, they surely have not already got rid of all they brought from Bantam. Lastly, they desire him not to detain the messenger more than two days, forgetting that they have often kept a ‘cassett’ [courier: <em>qāsid</em>] four or five months; however, ‘had I not bine promised my firmane today, your cassett had departed away yesterday.’ Advised them <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-202" data-page="202" data-folio="148" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n201_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 148">[p. 148]</button> in his last letter that ‘the King had graunted all my petticion and at takeing leave asked mee if I remembred ought elce. For the debt of Meir Mahmud, the Serkale, my speciall good freind, I forbeare to demaund till am enformed how it grew; and then from Bunder shall write him of it, being confident, if it bee a due debt, it wilbe paid on demaund. I am heartily glad your usage in Mesulapatam is soe good and that you injoy such priveledges as were graunted Mr. Joice. Tis not your kind useage of them hath occasioned it, for since my being heere severall complaints have bine made, insoemuch that I have bine tould you have made forffett the Kings firmand graunted Mr. Joice; for in that firmand wee are injoyned not to father any strangers goods, and yett very lately a horse which was brought upon the <em>Thomas</em>, belonging to an Armenian, must be taken into the Companies howse as the Companies. But this, and what past concerning Captain Weddall, the King hath freely forgiven; for, in confidence of a better goverment hereafter, he was pleased to swear by his Prophett that former injuries done him and his subjetts should bee remembred noe more.’ Agrees that, for the reasons they give, it would be dangerous for the <em>Thomas</em> to go to Malacca; but he commands them not to send her to Bantam until he arrives. Will not be able to bring them the 26,000 rials of eight expected, for he has received letters from Surat desiring him to abstain from drawing upon them for any money until the arrival of ships from England or from Mokha, as they are out of funds owing to the scarcity of rupees and the repayment to the Dutch of the 2,000 tūmāns which should have been paid in Persia. Cannot approve of the proposed remittance to Achin by way of Jambi, as secrecy is essential; so, unless it can be sent in the way he suggested, the money had better be kept for the present. Has not presented the account to Mīrzā Taqī, as he has asked him to recover 500 pagodas due to the Company from ‘our landlord’ and to take it as part of his debt. The enclosure will show the Company’s pleasure concerning Cogan, though he protests he is in no way ambitious of the post of Agent and would willingly serve elsewhere. Trusts that they will persevere in rectifying what they find amiss in Masulipatam and the subordinate factories. (<em>Signed copy. 3 pp.</em>)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-203"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="203" data-folio="149" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n202_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 149">[p. 149]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">The Golconda Farmāns of</span> 1639 (<em>O.C.</em> 1687, 1688).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-073" href="#sec-06-fn-073" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-073" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h3><h4 class="sec-h3">A.</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">‘God is merciefull and great. Yahamud Yale.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-074" href="#sec-06-fn-074" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-074" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> Firman or our letter of credence to all the subjects of my dominions. Soe far as the sunne exceeds all smaller lights, soe glorious am I above other Kings; to whome all Kings are united in freindshipp; a King not subject to fortune; from the begininge greate and hitherto continnewed. To all Govermours, Substitutes, Officers, Freeholders, or else whome it may concerne in these our roiallties and dominions. Know you that the glory of a King consists in the welfaire of his subjects, and their wellfare and benifitt imediatly (under God) accrews by comerse; to which purpose wee in our roiall favor have granted to all nations free egresse and regresse to our ports and dominions, that they may pray for the life of our Roiall Majestie. But in especiall the thrice vallient and worthy English captain, Andrew Cogan, repaireing to Our Highnes court and made peticion that, if it should seeme expedient in our gratious favor, to [<em>sic</em>] confirme the auncient priveledges confered on that nacion; which upon his peticion and our royall goodnesse wee have graunted, intreating him with all love and respect, [and] have given him leave to depart my royall court, with free leave and full power to negotiate his designes (?); what money, treasure, goods, or what elce he shall deliver to weavers, pai[nters], or any other, that his worke bee puntually performed and debts cleered; and further the Governours, or by what other name call’d, they shall not only suffer [him in] quiett, but alsoe doe their best to asist him, suffering neither goods nor mony to bee remaines without his espetiall leave; and that neither goods, persons, or servants be detained upon any pretence, but in all passages you rather helpe them; for of our gracious goodnes wee have freed them of all imposts, customes, duties, or by what other name you may call them. By all meanes molest them not, but intreate them kindly, as you tender my high displeasure, and that they may enjoy quiettnesse and pray for our <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-204" data-page="204" data-folio="150" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n203_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 150">[p. 150]</button> prosperitie.
