She reached Gombroon in safety and, leaving that port on April 12, returned on April 25, bringing silk, carpets, and money. Had then intended her to go to Bantam, but she was found to be quite unseaworthy. She was therefore brought up the river, and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-263" data-page="263" data-folio="209" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n262_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 209">[p. 209]</button> during the rains was thoroughly repaired at a cost of nearly 4,000 mahmūdīs. On October 19 she was speeded to Chaul to carry a crew, &amp;c., for a new vessel which Cogan had bought on his way to Goa for 10,000 'xeraphins'. This ship is of 300 tons burden and strongly built. Her owner, Francisco de Souza de Castro, would not have parted with her under as many rials; but he being still a prisoner at Achin, his wife and son-in-law have sold the vessel to raise means for his release. William Broadbent has been made master of her, with Richard Fitch as purser; and she has been named the <em>Supply</em>. As the price covered only the hull, two cables, and two anchors, she is to proceed to Goa, convoyed by the <em>Francis</em>, to be masted and fitted with all necessaries. The <em>Francis</em> is to return at once with coir, betel-nuts, and arrack for the <em>London's</em> use. Money and lead sent in her to Goa, consigned to William Pitt. When she comes back, she will probably be dispatched to 'Scinda' and thence to Gombroon. Possibly after her return she will be able to carry a second freight to Gombroon before the rains, and bring back Abbot with the proceeds of the goods he has sold. Enclose a copy of a letter from Walter Clark to Bantam describing the disaster to the <em>Comfort</em>. Several of her crew now go home in the <em>Discovery</em>; from them the Company may, if it is thought fit, recover a proportion of the money paid for their ransom. It is reported that 1,400 Malabars were killed in the action, 'whence wee should thinke the whole nation should bee terrified in the future from medling with us; but wee find the contrary, and hope to pay them at some time or other according to theire deservings. They have made us theire enemies, and therby induced us to enorder your sea comanders wherever they encounter them (if they can master them) to ruine them.'<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-129" href="#sec-06-fn-129" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-129" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Commend Walter Clark, not only for his resolute behaviour in the fight, but also for his sober and discreet comportment here, before he sailed in the <em>Michael</em>. They think that he deserves to be acquitted of what was paid for his ransom and to be given a gratuity towards his losses and some good employment in the future. The <em>Diamond</em>, which (as already stated) sailed for Persia on January 29, carried passengers and freight goods producing 6,466 mahmūdīs, a sum nearly equal to what she <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-264" data-page="264" data-folio="210" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n263_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 210">[p. 210]</button> cost the Company. From Gombroon she went to ‘Scinda’ with 700 tūmāns ‘in specie of abassees’, but arrived so late that she was forced to winter there, ‘wheare that malevolent aire swept away the greater part of 20 Englishmen which went hence on her to Persia.’ On hearing of this from Bornford, twenty of the <em>London's</em> crew were sent thither in the <em>Kit</em>; and both vessels returned to Swally on November 3 with sugar, sugar candy, and calicoes, besides a bale of Persian felts and two horses, all amounting to 80,635 mahmūdīs. On the 23rd ditto she was again dispatched to Persia with freight goods paying 5,514½ mahmūdīs. The <em>Michael</em> left Goa on February 17 and got back to Swally on March 22, bringing, besides freight goods, 20,038 rials of the money so long detained by the Portuguese. On her way hither she fetched Pitt from Rājāpur and Downham from Dābhol. No other suitable employment presenting itself, it was resolved to send her towards Mokha ‘to waylay the Mallavars in theire usuall returne from that port, that soe wee might vindicate, if possible, your losses by the <em>Comfort</em>’. Joseph Downham and Edward Harrison (formerly purser of the <em>Comfort</em>) were put into the <em>Michael</em> to look after the sale of some Deccan calicoes she had on board; while Walter Clark was deputed to assist her master. She sailed on April 18 in fine weather, but shortly after encountered a violent storm, which damaged her so much that she was forced to seek refuge in ‘Duffar<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-130" href="#sec-06-fn-130" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-130" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>’, on the coast of Arabia. Having thus lost the monsoon, she remained there until her cargo was sold and then proceeded to Gombroon. She was next to go to ‘Scinda’, where she is supposed to be at present. She is expected here about January 10, and they then propose to dispatch her in the following month to Basrā, returning in August or September by way of Gombroon. It will be better for the silk to be housed at the latter port during the rains than brought here for storage, for the Governor of Surat may at any time demand customs on it. Hope that the <em>Supply</em> will bring from Goa 100 or 200 tons of pepper; ‘for such a quantity (since they have no ships from Portugall this yeare, nor have not any left here to send thither) wee have desired of the V[ice]Roy, for [from?] whose affable and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-265" data-page="265" data-folio="211" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n264_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 211">[p. 211]</button> noble disposition and ready willingness to gratify our desires wee cannot expect lesse then performance. Being here arived, wee intend her to Persia with fraught goods, which (since the way by Candahar is stopped) so abound in Suratt that here are not vessells enough to transport them. Wee hope in one voiage to quit her cost; and being returned to send her to Bantam to bring thence pepper to stowe among other goods for England.' Last March the Governor of Surat presented to them a small frigate of from 30 to 40 tons, which they named the <em>Prosperous</em>; but within a month he demanded 1,000 rupees for her, and this sum they were obliged to pay. However, as she is new and well fitted, the price is deemed very cheap. In May she was sent to Rājāpur with Pitt and Tash, to sell some rūnās and clear accounts there. She had also on board some freight goods for Goa; and to that place her sottish master carried her first of all. When she attempted to leave that harbour for Rājāpur, she was nearly cast away on the bar, and her cargo was thoroughly soaked. As a result she was forced to winter at Goa, and to sell her damaged rūnās at a poor price. 'Since August she hath thrice assayed to come for Suratt, and hath bine as often inforced into Goa by the Mallabars, with whom although she fought on very unequall tearmes, yet she cleared herself with a great deale of reputation to those five Englishmen in her; for more she never had on board her, and in the first conflict fired one of three frigotts that assaulted her.' She is expected daily; and is then to accompany the <em>Michael</em> to Basrā. The <em>Discovery</em> reached Gombroon on January 14 last and got back to Swally February 18, bringing three horses, 1,050 tūmāns (in abbāsīs), and freight goods paying 12,436 lārīs. On March 14 she sailed for Mokha with calicoes and freight goods, Messrs. Wylde and Pearce being sent in charge of her cargo. The latter was invoiced at 180,323 mahmūdīs, while the amount received for freight was 20,497 mahmūdīs. She left Mokha on August 19 and reached Swally on September 21, bringing back more than half her goods unsold and money amounting to 61,865 mahmūdīs. 'In her returne, not long after she was cleare of the Bab[-ul-Mandab], they encountred <em>Eandrackt</em>, a ship of Diepe, comanded by one Regamont<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-131" href="#sec-06-fn-131" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-131" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, the same that three yeares since <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-266" data-page="266" data-folio="212" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n265_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 212">[p. 212]</button> pillaged the severall vessells of Dio; who, being invited on board, readily consented and was theare deteyned prisoner; whom his consorts would willingly have rescued; who, finding theire ship much nimbler then yours, both for saile and steerage, presumed to come neare enough to discharge divers great shott against the <em>Discovery</em> without doing the least harme. What hurt those the <em>Discovery</em> repaid her withall did is unknowne; yet judged important, because she so abruptly left them. It then appeared that they had not robbed any vessell; and that conception is since confirmed not only by Regamont, who was brought hither on the <em>Discovery</em>, but also by the severall safe arrivalls of as many vessells as belonged to this and Cambaiett ports.' Have treated their prisoner with all courtesy and have advanced him 428 mahmūdīs to buy necessaries. 'His ship was of burthen about 300 tonns, had 18 guns, and sayled excellently well, which best steaded her; otherwise she had also accompanied the <em>Discovery</em> hither, as her comander doth the <em>Discovery</em> to England.' His companions are supposed to have gone to the straits of the Red Sea, and doubtless will injure the English if they get a chance. The <em>Discovery</em> is now fully laden. Commend her mate, John Allison; but do not wish by this 'any way to disparage Mr. Minors his abillityes. His actions sufficiently speake his prayse, if his tounge were not long enough to boast them. Hee is only sicke of selfe love of his owne worth, which disease hath bine by your goodness so increased that now hee propounds unto his future imitation Captain Weddell's practize; which though hee hath spoken here, hee will not peradventure dare to wisper in England: that hee will rather desert your future imployment then bee againe directed by your President and Councell in India, or that his ship, stores, and men shall bee at theire disposure.' The <em>London</em> sailed for Persia on October 23, and is expected back by Christmas; in which case they hope to get her cargo for England on board by January 10. The <em>Thomas</em>, after spending nearly <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-267" data-page="267" data-folio="213" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n266_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 213">[p. 213]</button> twelve months on her way, reached Gombroon from Masulipatam on December 15, 1638. Her cloves were sold, though to poor profit, owing to the extraordinary lack of weight, concerning which they are making inquiries at Bantam. ‘From Persia the <em>Thomas</em> in March carried to Dabull 40 Persian horses belonging to that Kings embassador sent to the King of Gulcondah ; which having theare safely landed, she proceeded to Messl[i]pa[tam], arrived and was trimmed theare, and is since (the primo November) sent to Bantam with a carga[zon] of goods which cost you pagodas 5,845. 2. 1.’ Disposal of the goods returned from Mokha. Intend to forbear further trade with that port ‘till better marketts, or want of other imployment for your greater shipps to passe over the winter with, shall againe invite us thither. Your trade at Gombroone was not much better. The Grand Signiors invasion of Bagdatt and stoppage of all entercourse betweene Turky and Persia made it so.’ Yet there was a gain, in that all the sale proceeds were brought back to Surat or disposed of in accordance with the orders given there. This course will be continued, in spite of the calumnies of the Persia factors, ‘who repine cheifly because they cannot make your trade theirs, sell your goods to themselves in Gombroone at theire owne prizes, resell them at Spahan, and yet make you pay for theire transport thither. These courses (wee are certainely given to understand) have bine fraequently practized by them ; and all to mainteyne theire beastly ryott and debauchure.’ The freight book will show ‘how gainefull these fraughting voyages have bine unto you’ ; particularly the <em>London</em>, which earned in this way by her voyage to Persia 40,096 mahmūdīs. It is true that considerable trouble is caused, since the President is obliged to go to ‘the Maryne’ to superintend the lading of even a pinnace ; but this practice is so advantageous that they intend to continue it. Honywood returned from Persia in the <em>Discovery</em>, according to order, and remained here till July 11, when he died after nine days’ sickness, leaving no will ; his accounts are sent herewith. The only other casualty has been the killing by accident of James Congden ; particulars will be found in the account of the trial of John Perkins, the person responsible. The latter was acquitted ; but for this and other misgovernment he has been ordered ‘to serve before the masts’ aboard the <em>Discovery</em>. Will now briefly relate the proceedings <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-268" data-page="268" data-folio="214" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n267_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 214">[p. 214]</button> of the Dutch and the Portuguese, referring for details to 'the Presidents journall' [<em>missing</em>]. Not long after Methwold left Goa, the Dutch raised the blockade and departed for Ceylon. The Portuguese thereupon prepared 'to assist Damon against the Moores, who having overrunne all the country and destroyed the townes from which the Portugalls have their maintenance, they seated themselves before Damon [and] continued theare four or five months to very litle purpose, the Portugalls in many sallyes they made out of the towne having still the better of them. During this seige the caphilaes which were accustomed to goe to Cambaiett were withheld by the Portugalls; which this Governor perceiving, and that the continuance of this warr would ruine his port Cambaia, by the help of the President mediated a peace, and procured the coming of two eminent padres to confirme it. Yet ere this was perfected, that State had sent the present V[ice] Roy with 50 frigotts and 1,200 souldiers to succor Damon; and yet before hee arrived, through this Governors procuration, the Princes army was remooved, after that the Portugall inhabitants of Damon had obliged themselves to pay the Prince the same rent they were accustomed to give the Raja of that country, vizt. 60,000 m[ahmūdī]s, made to bee the quarter part of the provenue of that land. In which treaty the President, for the paines hee tooke, gayned from both those nations both respect and reputation; insomuch that when the two Jesuits arrived (who were lodged in your howse whilest they staid in towne, in imitation of theire like practize to us upon like occasions), sent to confirme the peace on the V[ice] Roies part, they were positively enjoyned in theire instruccions neither to propound or conclude ought without first consulting with the President.' Have already advised the death of the Viceroy and the succession of the present one.</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Last March the galleon which had arrived at Goa the previous October was laden and sent back to Portugal. 'This yeare they have no succour from Portugall; so that they are very much distrest and oppressed by the Hollanders at Goa, Zeiloan, and Mallacca; whither, and so forward to China, they againe desire to imploy two or three of your ships, and have to that purpose dispeeded a quallified person to treat therof; who arrived here the first current and departed sorrowfully for the fruitlesse voyage hee had made; for wee readily shewed our <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-269" data-page="269" data-folio="215" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n268_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 215">[p. 215]</button> disabilities to serve that State, when wee declared that one of your two shipps in these parts of India was fully laden for England and that the greatest part of the others lading awayted on the Maryne her returne from Persia. They are much distracted for want of releife from Portugall, which makes some of them say (even men of cheife note among them) that if the King of Spaine cares no more for them, they will put themselves under some other Princes protection who will affoard them readier and abler defence against the Hollanders. Of necessity they will bee inforct to desert some of theire forts to mainteyne those that are most usefull, and defencible the better. They are spread all alongst this coast and well may spare some of theire forts. Wee could heartily wish any one of them yours. If you had ships wherwithall to serve them to China, either strong enough of themselves to resist the Dutches insolent violent manner of searching for Portugalls goods or inforced by His Majestyes commission (which yet wee thinke they would hardly vaile to, in respect of the dominion they have in these seas), wee would not doubt to procure from them such conditions and performances as would bee very advantageous to you; for wee beleive they would readily subscribe to furnish you with pepper, cinamon, and as much freedome and security in some of theire forts (if not the fort itself) as wee can desire or they themselves owne. That State, for repairing theire Armada Dalta Boarda<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-132" href="#sec-06-fn-132" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-132" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, or to supply the nomber of those three gallions the Dutch fired, have borrowed of theire Misericordia<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-133" href="#sec-06-fn-133" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-133" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, a monastery in Goa, 300,000 xeraphins or 150,000 rials, with which having built and fitted four other new gallions, which they say shall equall the former in bigness, they intend to encounter the Dutch the ensueing yeare, who in the interim (as they doe) make what havocke they list.' The first Dutch vessels to arrive here after the departure of the <em>Swan</em> were two ‘fluicks<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-134" href="#sec-06-fn-134" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-134" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>’, from ‘Teiwan<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-135" href="#sec-06-fn-135" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-135" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup>, neare China’. These landed great quantities of commodities of those parts, including 200 chests of Japan silver, 4 chests of China gold, and <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-270" data-page="270" data-folio="216" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n269_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 216">[p. 216]</button> 200 bales of China silk, &amp;c. The vessels then proceeded to Persia. Their ‘Buss’ [<em>see</em> p. 54] returned from Gombroon and sailed with a cargo of indigo about the end of April. Then also came one of the ‘fluicks’ from Persia, and was thereupon dispatched to Batavia. On May 4 two other Dutch ships arrived from Gombroon, one of which had gone direct from Batavia to the Red Sea, commanded by ‘Vanderbrooke’ [<em>see</em> p. 142] and laden with spices and Chinese goods.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-136" href="#sec-06-fn-136" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-136" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Three Dutchmen were left behind with some merchandise; they sold their goods and came hither on the <em>Discovery</em>. These ships sailed on May 20. ‘In Persia arrived from Battavia two shipps, and one Adam Westerholt<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-137" href="#sec-06-fn-137" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-137" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> to direct theire affaires theare; who since his arrivall hath done litle, although it was thought that a man of his eminence came about some more important business then is as yet discovered.’ The Dutch are supposed to have exported from Persia this year between 500 and 600 bales of silk. ‘This Kings sonne, when his army was before Damon, writt a letter to the Dutch Generall and required assist of ships by sea whilest his souldiers by land indeavored Damons surprizall; for which service and to defray shipps expences these spetious offers were presented, vizt. 200,000 rupees in specie, halfe the amount of others customes, free import and export of theire owne goods, and the quarter part of that countryes provenue; which offers theire Generall slights, judging them (and that truly) propounded only to bee kept whilest without them the Prince cannot keepe Damon.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-138" href="#sec-06-fn-138" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-138" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup> On Zeiloan they have this yeare taken another fort by composition from the Portugalls, called Triconomela<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-139" href="#sec-06-fn-139" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-139" role="doc-noteref">4</a></sup>, and in it 50,000 pounds weight of cinamon, 10,000 pounds weight of wax, and 15 small iron guns; in liewe wherof they are to set free on shoare at Negapatan the base cowardly rascalls that well might and yet durst not keepe it. This yeare againe they are come thither with six ships and (tis said) 2,000 men, comanded by Phillip Lucas, Director-Generall and Second of Councell to the Generall [<em>see the previous volume</em>, pp. 6, <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-271" data-page="271" data-folio="217" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n270_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 217">[p. 217]</button> 142–3]. They intend now to assault Columbo, and it is thought they will carry it; and then the Portugalls may bid adiew to Zeiloan, whilest the Dutch may boast of being masters of all the spice countries in the universe, pepper excepted. Before Mallacca report saith they have had these six months ten saile of ships, awayting the King of Achyns coming with his army to assault it by land whilest they ply it to seaward; and thither it was the Portugalls would have had your <em>Discovery</em> carry rice and other provision of victualls and munition, as powder, shot, guns, and the like. They indeavor theire utmost, and allready, in conceipt of its surprizall, boast that if they can make that theire owne, no ship shall passe to China or the south seas without theire leave. A Portugall vessell returned from China, being forced through foule weather to anchor under that titulary fort Armagon, put herself under your protection, anchoring as neare the fort as she could swim; from whence your people theare resident sent him an English flag, which hee fixed on his topmasthead. Yet the Dutch, having newes therof at Palicatt, sent two vessells of theirs to fetch her thence; which your servants in vaine forbad and protested against them for the damage that should ensue to you, whose vessell and goods they now pretended were become yours; which the Dutch slighted, and the Portugall, seing noe other remedy left (having before the Dutches coming doubted [or?] rather expected such issue, and therfore landed the best part of his best goods), set fire to the powder that was in the vessell and soe burnt her; in which predicament the Dutch left her and went to Pollicatt. At Goa this last September seven Dutch ships arrived from Battavia met theare two others which had bine speeded before them to Vingerla. These jointly entred Old Goa River, wheare they found two great gallions (not yet freed of their strawe winter coverings) upon the caryne. These with small resistance and as little trouble they fired; neare unto which a third gallion, called the <em>Boun Jesus</em>, whearin one Alvaro de Souza de Tavaro comanded, which was lately come thither to fitt and fetch away the other two, entred fight with the Dutch and mainteynd it untill the Dutch made meanes to cutt the burning gailions cables and so give them more liberty to drive upon the <em>Boun Jesus</em>; whoe, being lighted from the others neighbour flames, consumed with them, and so all three perished. The <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-272" data-page="272" data-folio="218" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n271_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 218">[p. 218]</button> other[s] were called <em>St. Sebastian</em> and <em>Sta. Boa Ventura</em>.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-140" href="#sec-06-fn-140" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-140" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> Forty Portugalls in this fight were taken prisoners; the rest, that could not swim, could not escape those irresistable elements, but by them perished. This done, the Dutch, without any great hurt received from the fort Mormagon, which had neither men nor munition in it worth mentioning, issued out of that river and came and anchored before New Goa in theire wonted birthing, attending the carracks or gallions arrivall from Portugall untill the midle of November, when they departed to the southwards, and are thought to bee gone to Zeiloan to help theire fellowes. From Battavia for this place three ships, comanded by Vanderbrooke, were speeded; two of which, vizt. second and third ship, arrived the 30th October and, having landed here saponwood, sandall, nuttmeggs, elephants teeth, quicksilver, vermelion, and 10½ broad clothes . . . supplied that vacancy hence with fraught goods and set saile for Persia the 23th November, whither the greater part of their lading (continued on board) is designed. The admirall of these three ships (whearin the treasure is) is not yet heard of. This yeare they have not bought many goods, occasioned through theire being so much indebted here and in Ahmudabad. Report saith they owe 1,200,000 m[ahmūdī]s. Vanderman<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-141" href="#sec-06-fn-141" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-141" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup>, theire Generall, is continued three yeare[s] longer in his kingdome; and therfore Phillip Lucas, his second, who had thought to have succeeded him, this yeare goes home. The Director of theire business here goes this yeare also to Battavia, and his second (which was cheife at Agra) succeeds him [<em>cf.</em> p. 240]. The Danes have had this passed yeare a ship of theirs in Persia, laden with spices and Bengala goods. They arrived to a very bad markett, for they sold but a few goods at low prizes and were inforced to recarry the remayning greater part. At Messl[i]pa[tam] they are very much indebted and no way able to make sattisfaccion. When their owne creditt could not procure them any monies, your kindhearted servants, Clarke and Hudson, tooke up money at 3 per cent. interest upon your creditt and lent it them again at 2½ per cent.’ ‘Richard Fitch and his crew, having after the <em>Blessings</em> departure bine deteyned in Aden by that trecherous Governor about two months, were by the Emome of <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-273" data-page="273" data-folio="219" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n272_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 219">[p. 219]</button> Mocha his command inlarged; whence they came to Mocha, and Fitch stayed theare untill the <em>Discovery</em> came thence. The other came to Persia on the Dutch ship, and thence hither on the <em>Francis</em>.’ Particulars of account books now sent. A young man named John Stanford returns in this ship, after serving the Company for nearly seven years. He went home before as purser’s mate in the <em>Jonas</em>, and returned hither in the <em>Mary</em>. He was then taken ashore and employed at Broach and ‘Scinda’ as assistant to Spiller. This last year he was made purser of the <em>Francis</em>, and in this capacity, as in all others, he has served with care, industry, and honesty. Recommend his re-engagement, either as purser or factor. Request a yearly supply of three or four ‘able, active, civill young men’; more still, ‘if you intend to reassume your Persian trade.’ They will not, however, send newcomers to that country, ‘least that aire and goverment should disease and ruine them.’ As they have now so many small vessels employed, it is necessary that they should be furnished yearly with a chest of drugs and ‘chirurgery ware’. They need an able and judicious ‘chirurgion’ for this factory, who may also distribute the drugs to the various vessels and check their disposal. If more and better ‘chirurgions mates’ were employed on the great ships, they might be utilized in the small vessels when they could be spared. Extra sailors should be sent in each ship for employment in the pinnaces, for if they had not laid up the <em>Blessing</em>, ‘which yet lyes still at Goa unrequired by the Portugalls,’ they could not have found men enough to sail the smaller vessels. At the request of Duarte Fernandez Correa, who has helped them much in their correspondence with the Portuguese, they forward a chest which he desires to have delivered to Mr. Methwold or Mr. Spurstow for transmission to Venice. Send an abstract of the Company’s estate in these parts at the arrival of the <em>London</em>; also a later account in which allowance is made for the cost of the return cargo of that ship. It will be seen how little will be left for future investments, and how impossible it is to avoid fresh borrowing. <em>PS.</em>—Enclose copies of letters just received from Masulipatam. Will do their best to remedy the deplorable state of affairs in those parts. Intend to write to Bantam, requesting that Clark and Hudson be sent hither to clear up discrepancies and omissions discovered since their departure. Should they or Pinson have been <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-274" data-page="274" data-folio="220" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n273_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 220">[p. 220]</button> sent home already, the Company is advised to seize their estates pending further information. As for Ivy, they think it right to report a statement of his (advised in a letter from Masulipatam dated September 14) that not only in Surat but also ‘in England by the Companies servants hee would have a letter pend’ as hee pleasd for 10 or 20<em>s.</em>’ They leave the Company to punish ‘such transcendent villany’. (49 <em>pp. Received June</em> 9, 1640.)</p></div><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p"><em>Enclosure</em>: Invoice of the cargo of the <em>Discovery</em>, consisting of 342 bales of Persian silk, 500 of Sarkhej and 37 of Biāna indigo, 399 of sugar, 32 of sugar candy, 100 of ginger, 104 of saltpetre, 319 of piece-goods, and 127 of cotton yarn. Total cost, 816,213 mahmūdīs 12⅛ pice. (9¾ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Lists of Papers, etc., from Surat per the</span> <em>Discovery</em> (<em>O.C.</em> 1721, 1726, 1726 I).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Three varying lists of books, accounts, and letters sent home in the ship. (3¾ <em>pp.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">President Fremlen and John Wylde at Swally Marine to the Company, December</span> 12, 1639 (<em>O.C.</em> 1727).</h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">After the packet had been sealed, 920 long red beads were brought to them from Cambay. Though the number is small and the quality is poor, they send them by this ship, and will provide more and better by the <em>London</em>, including a proportion of round beads, which, according to Methwold, are preferred in Madagascar. The present consignment cost seven rupees per hundred. The <em>Discovery</em> is now under sail. (½ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><h4 class="sec-h3"><span class="sc">Andrew Cogan and other Factors at Masulipatam to the Factors in Persia, December</span> 14, 1639 (<em>O.C.</em> 1729).<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-142" href="#sec-06-fn-142" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-142" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup></h4><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Having received orders from Surat to charge Persia by exchange for what money is needed, they hereby notify that they have drawn on them for 11,200 abbāsīs in favour of Nākhudā Mullā Hasan Alī. The money was very welcome, for ‘our credite at present is utterly <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-275" data-page="275" data-folio="221" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n274_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 221">[p. 221]</button> lost; being railed at every moment by one or other for moneys, insomuch as the Agente is forste to keepe house as a prisnor to avoyd clamores of our creditors. The 20th of the last moneth wee, the Hollanders and Daines were commanded to meete the King two dayes journie from this place; where with at least 5,000<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-143" href="#sec-06-fn-143" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-143" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> people he arived the 27th and continawed untell the 8th corant, to the allmost undoeing of this parte of the country. And to saye trueth, his coming was purposely for presentes; but he cam shorte of his exspectacion beyond measure, for wee veryly beleeve he scarce got the half of 600,000 pagodas promised by the Serkale. For wee for our partes could present him with nothing, haveing naither moneys, goodes, nor credite (as before); which by letters and ellse wee posest the King withall. Indeede he sawe with his eyes wee spake and writt trueth, to the shame of our nation, whoe he thought (as he sayed) drove a trade for many milliones of pagodas. For after hee had beene in towne two dayes, wee were commanded out of our howse to a gardaine some two myles from the towne and to leave all our chistes open to vew, under pretence to buy som toyes. In our ould rotten house he and som 100 of his woemen spent som four or six howres to vew and revew what wee had, which in totall wee could not vallew to 2,000 pagodas; for wee were injoyned to leave a price upon all our goodes whattever. The like was done by the Duch and Danes; and in fine, to make us the more redicullous, wee were peremtorylie commanded to send our good[es] into the marquitt for salle, but noe mankind with them; and women wee had none. In conclusion, after three dayes our goodes were retorned againe, and not anythinge sould of the Companies, which was onely 10 cande[es] sandell [and] 15 tubbs china. The Danes are in as bad a predicament as wee. But the Duch are the men in esteeme, for they gave him in rubies etc. (as tis credable[y] reported) to the amounte of 300<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-144" href="#sec-06-fn-144" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-144" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> pagodas, and promised that with the first of the monzoone to give him more an eliphant and five Persian horses; for which their giftes and promises the Kinge have given them new firmandes<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-145" href="#sec-06-fn-145" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-145" role="doc-noteref">3</a></sup>, their ould being out of dade [<em>sic</em>], to trade freely in all his dominions and onely payeing 3,000 pagodas per annum. This was putt often into our dish by <button type="button" class="folio-trigger folio-turn" id="page-276" data-page="276" data-folio="222" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n275_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect original scan for page 222">[p. 222]</button> severall Persians nere the King; whoe likewise tould [us] that the Danes, which paye all duties and are indebted one-third parte more then wee are, have promised by writing (because not otherwaies able) to give the Kinge, upon arivall of their shipe from Europe, 2,000 pagodas. And in conclusion wee were tould plainely wee [must ?] either give or promise, but moneys he would none [<em>sic</em>. ? have] upon any tearmes; whereupon wee were upon the matter compelled to promise that upon the arivall of our shipes from Suratt and England to present him with some rarities to the amount of 3,000 pagodas; and therefore wee shall desire (and hope our Presidente will soe enorder) that you send us some good Persian horses, which are as much or more in esteme then any thinge that can be presented him. Upon this jounke is laden for this Kings accompt the goods mencioned in the inclosed; which he desireth maye passe free of customes. The request is not unreasonable, in respect wee paye none here; and therefore you may not (unlesse you will indanger the losse of our priveliges) denie it, not allthough twere farre more then it is. It wilbe needles to saye what our priveliges are; but if the Companie follow this trade, they might yeerely invest with ease 300,000 pagodas, which at 25 per cent. all duties will amount to a round some of moneys, and all this and more wee are freed of; therefore againe, in hopes that now, this factory being under Suratt, the Company will follow it better then foremerly, and thereby make it a benifishall trade, doe desire you not onely to passe the goodes belonging to the Kinge but use the Nokoda with respect, he being a kinde of a churchman<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-146" href="#sec-06-fn-146" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-146" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup>, and one very well beloved of the Serkalee [<em>sic</em>], whoe upon the matter governeth the whole kingdom.' Send in the said junk, on the Company's account, three bales of piece-goods which cost 475 pagodas 3 fanams. These are 'such as the merchants that come to your porte buy in great quantaties, and such as the Duch are intended to deale in, for their firmand lately procured (as above) gives them leave to settell a factory where these sortes are made'.<sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-147" href="#sec-06-fn-147" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-147" role="doc-noteref">2</a></sup> Should those now forwarded produce good profit, they will look out for more. (<em>Copy.</em> 2 <em>pp. Received in London November</em> 20, 1640.)</p></div><div class="page-rail-marker" id="page-277"><button type="button" class="folio-trigger" data-page="277" data-folio="223" data-scan="https://archive.org/download/englishfactories06fost/page/n276_w1400.jpg" aria-label="Inspect scan for page 223">[p. 223]</button></div><h3 class="sec-h2"><span class="sc">List of Books delivered to Gerald Pinson at Bantam</span><sup class="fn-ref"><a id="ref-sec-06-fn-148" href="#sec-06-fn-148" data-fn-target="sec-06-fn-148" role="doc-noteref">1</a></sup> [<span class="sc">December</span> 31, 1639?] (<em>O.C.</em> 1750).</h3><div class="prose-unit"><p class="prose-p">Journals and ledgers for Masulipatam, Petapoli, Vīravāsaram, Armagon, Golconda, ‘Harrerpore’ and ‘Baldesare’ [Balasore], ranging from 1636 to 1639. Also the Masulipatam steward’s book for 1637–38. (¾ <em>p.</em>)</p></div><section class="apparatus-zone" aria-label="Footnotes"><h2 class="apparatus-heading">Critical Apparatus &amp; Notes</h2><ol class="apparatus-list"><li id="sec-06-fn-001" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-001" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Cogan, Breton, Robinson, and Wylde. Methwold has signed also (after Fremlen).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-002" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-002" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘The Cinamon of Malabar is called Canella de Mato or wilde Cinamon, and is forbidden to be carried into Portingale’ (Linschoten’s <em>Voyage</em>, ed. Hakl. Soc., vol. ii. p. 77). Teixeira (ed. Hakl. Soc., p. 236) speaks of cinnamon grown in jungles (<em>matos</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-003" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-003" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>See The Court Minutes of the East India Company, 1635–39</em>, p. 217.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-004" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-004" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Bāndra, nine miles north of Bombay. Fryer (p. 70) describes the Jesuit college at that place.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-005" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-005" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the introduction to the previous volume, p. xxv.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-006" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-006" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>battā</em>, 'exchange allowance'; see the previous volume, p. 68.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-007" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-007" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Muizz-ul-Mulk, better known as Mīr Mūsā (see p. 109).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-008" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-008" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">By repeating the seizure of Indian shipping.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-009" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-009" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sānti Dās, for whom see a note on p. 196 of the last volume.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-010" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-010" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently what is meant is a bale (literally a score: <em>korī</em>) conveyed by sea (<em>daryā</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-011" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-011" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See Mandelslo's account (English edition, p. 16). Either Sūr or Suhār, both ports on the coast of Omān.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-012" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-012" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly Hind. <em>phūsī</em>, chaff or rubbish.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-013" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-013" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>sajjī</em>, natron, impure carbonate of soda.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-014" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-014" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Kiswa</em>, or veil of black silk which covers the Kaba (the shrine of the Black Stone) at Mecca. This is renewed annually, and the discarded covering then becomes the perquisite of the keepers of the mosque. Once in seven years the <em>Kiswa</em> is sent in its entirety to the Sultan of Turkey (Wollaston's <em>Sword of Islam</em>, p. 352). The <em>Burqa</em>, or curtain of the door of the Kaba, is appropriated by the Grand Sharif of Mecca. Jahāngīr notes in his memoirs that in 1607 he received from the Sharif ‘the curtain of the door of the Kaba’ (<em>Tūzuk-i-Jahāngīrī</em>, translated by Rogers, p. 133). On the occasion referred to in the text the Governor of Surat appears to have secured the <em>Kiswa</em> for presentation to Shāh Jahān, and to have induced the English merchants to pay the expenses of transporting it with due ceremony from Surat to the court.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-015" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-015" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For Portuguese accounts of Weddell's proceedings see the <em>Lisbon Transcripts: Doc. Remett.</em>, book 41, ff. 191–240, and book 43, f. 37. A brief narrative is given in the <em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series iii. vol. iii. O 6.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-016" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-016" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A copy of the general letter sent home by her, dated at Macao December 19, 1637, will be found at f. 218 of <em>Rawlinson MS.</em> A 299 (Bodleian Library).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-017" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-017" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The Dutch heard at Goa that Peter Vandam, the second in command at Bhatkal, had murdered his chief, John Fortune, with two other Englishmen, and had fled to Goa with the factory goods and money, but had there been apprehended in March, 1638 (<em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. ix. nos. 350, 358). It would seem that, on learning from the <em>Katharine</em> that the settlement was deserted, Weddell determined to proceed thither at once; for, according to a letter from the Viceroy (<em>Doc. Remett.</em>, book 43, f. 39), he went from Cochin to Bhatkal and re-established the factory there, promising the King of Ikkeri, who was disposed to demur, that the merchants should depart in September, if required to do so.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-018" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-018" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For Portuguese negotiations with the Ikkeri chief on this subject see <em>Lisbon Transcripts: Doc. Remett.</em>, book 40, f. 138, book 43, f. 39, and book 44, f. 253. In the last of these, written in March, 1639, the Viceroy announces that Antonio Borges has prevailed upon Vīra Bhadra to expel the English and pull down their factory.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-019" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-019" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>San Juan de Dios</em>, burnt March 1, 1638 (N.S.) (<em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. xi. nos. 350, 358).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-020" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-020" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">On April 10 (N.S.).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-021" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-021" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Nossa Senhora da Oliveira</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-022" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-022" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>São Bento</em> and <em>Nossa Senhora da Conceição</em> reached Goa November 6 N.S. (October <span class="folio-turn">[p. 116]</span> 27 O.S.). The newly built galleon was the <em>São Bonaventura (Lisbon Transcripts: Doc. Remett.</em>, book 44, no. 250).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-023" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-023" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Under Antonio Caen. The fleet left Batavia July 28 N.S. (<em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. xi. nos. 350, 358, 360).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-024" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-024" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See a note on p. 326 of the 1630–33 volume. Hatch, it may be mentioned, belonged to Wingham (in Kent), which was near the home of the Oxendens.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-025" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-025" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">On Mandelslo’s arrival in England the Company at first decided to make him pay for his passage, but afterwards (at Methwold’s suggestion) relinquished this claim; whereupon Mandelslo gave 5<em>l.</em> to the poor-box in acknowledgement of the Company’s kindness (<em>Court Minutes, 1635–39</em>, p. 358; <em>1640–43</em>, pp. 17, 23).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-026" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-026" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">This was of course the well-known Count Wallenstein. Mandelslo’s connexion with him seems to be a fact hitherto unknown.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-027" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-027" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See also Mandelslo's account in his <em>Travels</em>. He gives details of most of the incidents mentioned in the log.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-028" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-028" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">See his trial in <em>Marine Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. iv. no. 16, p. 34.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-029" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-029" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For a copy of the consultation see <em>O.C.</em> 1675.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-030" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-030" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The consultation is recorded in <em>O.C.</em> 1677.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-031" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-031" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">See <em>O.C.</em> 1680. All three of these consultations are also recorded in <em>Marine Records</em>, <em>Miscellaneous</em>, vol. iv. no. 16.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-032" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-032" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">He had commanded the <em>Planter</em> in the first fleet sent out by the Association; and before that he had been for several years in the service of the Company.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-033" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-033" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">See Birkdale's log (p. 170). Mandelslo states that there was another ship in the harbour, belonging to the Company and homeward-bound (possibly from Bantam). Neither Bayley nor Birkdale says anything of this.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-034" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-034" aria-label="Return to text">6.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mandelslo's narrative would lead one to suppose that they actually touched at St. Helena; but this is partly the fault of his editor.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-035" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-035" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently the Joseph Teigee mentioned on p. 119. In the record of the consultation, however, he is called ‘Joseph Lussia’ (<em>Marine Records, Miscellaneous</em>, vol. iv. no. 16).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-036" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-036" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">After a consultation (<em>Ibid.</em>, p. 43). The coach was an erection on the quarter-deck, forming a cabin with the poop for roof. Such tophamper impaired the steering qualities of the vessel.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-037" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-037" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Leachland had married a native woman (p. 119 of the 1624–29 volume), and upon his death the factors made a small allowance to her and her young daughter (p. 61 of the previous <span class="folio-turn">[p. 123]</span> volume). The Company ordered the latter to be sent to England (<em>Court Minutes</em>, 1635–39, p. 120), but apparently the mother would not part with her child.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-038" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-038" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably the Kolī chief of Jawhār is meant. ‘Sarceta’ has been identified with Sávta, six miles east of Dāhānu. For the <em>chauth</em> paid by the Portuguese to this chief see <em>Hobson-Jobson</em>, 2nd edition, p. 215.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-039" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-039" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>See Lisbon Transcripts : Doc. Remett.</em>, book 45, f. 203.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-040" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-040" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A marginal note (made in London) reckons the lārī at tenpence.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-041" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-041" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">These details throw some light upon the organization of Courteen's Association, concerning which very little is known.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-042" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-042" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The portions of this letter not relating to India have been much condensed. That it was addressed to Fremlen is shown in <em>O.C.</em> 1725, 1726 <em>infra</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-043" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-043" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably the captain, James Slade, who had died on June 2, 1636 (see <em>supra</em>, p. 10, and p. 305 of the previous volume). Both he and Fremlen appear to have been connected with the parish of St. Leonard, Shoreditch.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-044" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-044" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This proverbial phrase, meaning to allow oneself to be cajoled, is not infrequent in writers of the time (cf. Shakespeare's <em>Winter's Tale</em>, i. 2). According to the Dutch (<em>Hague Transcripts</em>, series i. vol. xi. nos. 350, 358) Weddell declared on oath that his vessels had no Portuguese passengers or merchandise on board.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-045" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-045" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">His name is given as ‘Joseph Pinto Pereiro’. This document is wrongly dated 1638</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-046" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-046" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The place is not stated, but it was probably aboard the <em>Discovery</em> at Swally.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-047" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-047" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mandelslo describes this tank as a rectangular piece of water, five feet deep, and surrounded by a stone wall. On one side was a pleasure-house, in which one could eat and drink.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-048" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-048" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The record is signed by the President and the rest of the Court. The jury also sign the entry relating to their verdict; and two of the witnesses confirm their evidence in the same manner.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-049" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-049" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The distance, by the route taken, is about 440 miles: hence the <em>kos</em> is here reckoned at about a mile and a half.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-050" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-050" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">For Samāna (not far from Patiāla) and its famous calico see the 1618–21 volume, p. xxi.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-051" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-051" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">The fruit (<em>harrā</em> or <em>harlā</em>) of <em>Terminalia chebula</em> (<em>Myrobalans</em>), made into a conserve with sugar. This is probably the explanation of Tavernier's <em>arlet</em> (Ball's ed., vol. ii. p. 20).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-052" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-052" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Sal-ammoniac (<em>nausadar</em>). Finch (<em>Purchas</em>, vol. i. p. 431) noticed the 'sal-armoniake pits' at Thānesar.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-053" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-053" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">Not identified. Below it is called 'Serwerpore'.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-054" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-054" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">See the 1618-21 volume, p. 193.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-055" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-055" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly a copyist's error for <em>chautārs</em>, a kind of cotton cloth mentioned in the <em>Āīn</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-056" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-056" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Korīa: see the 1630-33 volume, p. 325.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-057" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-057" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pers. <em>lashkar</em>, a camp or army. The imperial encampment is here meant.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-058" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-058" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably Darbēlo, 12 miles north of Naushahro. But it is below Bukkur, not above, as Bornford seems to imply.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-059" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-059" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Būbak, 9 miles west of Sehwān.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-060" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-060" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Possibly Sann, on the river, about 30 miles south of Sehwān.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-061" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-061" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hāla Kandī, or Old Hāla, about 30 miles north of Hyderabad (Sind).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-062" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-062" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mr. G. P. Tate writes: ‘The Samidas can be no other than the Samija or Samejo, a numerous clan or tribe in Sind of the present day.’</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-063" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-063" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This is the original letter, endorsed as received at Surat (from the Viceroy) on June 8, 1639.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-064" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-064" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">He was bound for Bantam, to take up the post of President.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-065" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-065" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For these and previous negotiations on the same subject see the <em>Lisbon Transcripts: Doc. Remett.</em>, book 40, ff. 231, 375, and book 41, f. 11.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-066" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-066" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pieter van den Broeck, who has figured in previous volumes as the Dutch chief at Surat. After his arrival at Batavia he was sent to assist in the siege of Malacca, and died there on Dec. 1, 1640 N.S. (<em>Dagh-Register</em>, 1640–41, p. 161; Valentyn, vol. v. p. 341).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-067" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-067" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">There is a signed copy among the <em>O.C. Duplicates</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-068" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-068" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">San Thomés: see the previous volume, p. 99.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-069" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-069" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Dabīr</em> (Secretary) Mīrzā Taqī.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-070" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-070" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Eagle</em>. On the same page is a copy of a note from Cogan to the unknown captain, welcoming him and desiring him to follow any directions he may receive from the Masulipatam factors.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-071" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-071" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Unless the contrary is stated, ‘Winter’ always in these pages means Thomas Winter (not Edward).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-072" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-072" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Masulipatam: cf. p. 72, and see note on p. 221 of the 1622–23 volume.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-073" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-073" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">In Cogan’s handwriting.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-074" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-074" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Yā Muhammad! Yā Alī!</em> i.e. ‘O Muhammad! O Alī!’ This was the usual invocation prefixed to such documents at Golconda: cf. Havart’s <em>Op en Ondergang van Cormandel</em>, part ii. pp. 101, 103.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-075" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-075" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>Rabīa-us-sānī</em>, the fourth month of the Muhammadan year.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-076" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-076" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Arabic <em>san</em>, a year.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-077" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-077" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">i.e. the town of Mahmūd Shāh II of the Bahmanī dynasty, by whom Rājahmundry was taken from the Rājā of Orissa in 1470.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-078" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-078" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">On p. 162 Cogan says that three of these farmāns were issued.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-079" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-079" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The future Sir Edward Winter, Governor of Fort St. George. He was born in 1622 or 1623, and left England for the East while still a child (1630), no doubt in the charge of his elder brother Thomas (see p. 26).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-080" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-080" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Enclosed in Day’s letter of August 27. For another copy see the <em>O.C. Duplicates</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-081" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-081" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">For other copies see the <em>O.C. Duplicates</em> and <em>O.C.</em> 1694.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-082" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-082" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘in my name’ (<em>O.C.</em> 1694).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-083" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-083" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">A second copy forms part of <em>O.C.</em> 1694, and there is a third among the <em>O.C. Duplicates</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-084" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-084" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘Delivered by himselfe, 3d September, 1639.’ In the MS. the letter is dated July 27, which is obviously wrong.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-085" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-085" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Evidently the alleged sale to the English was a mere blind.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-086" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-086" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Malaya, for whom see p. 358 of the 1624–29 volume.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-087" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-087" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Printed in <em>The Founding of Fort St. George</em> (p. 7). The document is headed: ‘The firman granted Mr. Day for priviledges in Medrasspatam by the Nague Damela Vintutedra.’ This copy is in Cogan’s handwriting, and from the date of receipt in the endorsement (October 22, 1639) it appears to have been the one forwarded to Bantam. There are two other contemporary copies, viz. <em>O.C.</em> 1695, which is endorsed by Cogan, and one forming part of Cogan’s defence (<em>O.C.</em> 1751). These have been collated with the text and any variations of importance noted. The only one of the three versions bearing a date is that in <em>O.C.</em> 1751, which has ‘July 22, 1639’ appended. This is evidently wrong, but we may perhaps infer that it would be correct if ‘August’ were substituted; compare the similar mistake in the date of the covering letter.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-088" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-088" aria-label="Return to text">4.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘Damela Vintutedro Nague’ (<em>O.C.</em> 1695); ‘Damela Vintadedo Naique’ (<em>O.C.</em> 1751). In <em>O.C.</em> 1705 the name is given as ‘Damela Vincatedree’. For the correct form see the introduction.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-089" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-089" aria-label="Return to text">5.</a> <span class="fn-body">The other two versions read ‘in and att’.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-090" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-090" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘ this ’ in the other two.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-091" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-091" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘ inhabitants ’ (<em>O.C.</em> 1751) ; ‘ substitutes ’ (<em>O.C.</em> 1695). The last is the most probable reading.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-092" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-092" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">The other copies have ‘ setled ’</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-093" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-093" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body"><em>O.C.</em> 1751 adds : ‘or shall com to trade.’</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-094" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-094" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Messrs. Day, Peniston, Morris, and Winter. For a second copy see <em>O.C.</em> 1699.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-095" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-095" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Another copy (in Cogan’s hand) forms <em>O.C.</em> 1698, and there is a third among the <em>O.C. Duplicates</em> under the latter number.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-096" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-096" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This was incorrect. The <em>Thomas</em> left Goa on April 29 (see p. 142).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-097" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-097" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Messrs. Day, Peniston, Winter, Rogers, Greenhill, Tompkins, and Morley. The letter appears to have been the outcome of a consultation held the previous day (<em>see</em> p. 165). A second copy forms <em>O.C.</em> 1702. A second copy forms <em>O.C.</em> 1703.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-098" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-098" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Pedro da Silva died June 24, 1639 (N.S.). Antonio Telles did not get back to Goa until October 4 (N.S.), when he at once took over charge (as Governor) from the Archbishop of Goa, who had been acting meanwhile. Telles remained in charge until the arrival of his brother-in-law, João da Silva Tello de Menezes, Conde de Aveiras, who had been dispatched from Europe as Viceroy. The date of the transfer of office was September 21, 1640 N.S. (<em>Lisbon Transcripts: Doc. Remett.</em>, book 47, f. 6).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-099" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-099" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘Dellivered in the presence of Thomas Morris, Roger Wright, George Sephton, and Edward Hemingway.’ Like all the documents under this number, it is in Cogan’s handwriting. The bundle is endorsed as received (at Bantam ?) October 22, 1639. A second copy will be found under <em>O.C.</em> 1703 A, and a third among the <em>O.C. Duplicates</em>.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-100" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-100" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Printed (in part) in <em>The Founding of Fort St. George</em> (p. 9). Ivy signs apart from the rest, to avoid an acknowledgement of Cogan’s primacy.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-101" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-101" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">It is clear from later references that this should be ‘2,000’.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-102" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-102" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">This was signed by Cogan himself, Day, Peniston, Winter, Rogers, Greenhill, Humphrey Tompkins, and Thomas Morley. Another copy, dated September 5, forms <em>O.C.</em> 1704.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-103" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-103" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Left by Weddel at Achin as head of the factory founded there for Courteen’s Association.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-104" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-104" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The <em>Nākhudā</em> (shipmaster) Nūra. He is described in the endorsement as ‘servant to the Sirkayle’, and it is stated that he left Masulipatam the following day.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-105" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-105" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">He was master's mate. The ship was commanded by Matthew Wills.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-106" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-106" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">This piece of ground was probably given by the Sultan of Johanna to Captain Christopher Brown when the latter called at the island in the summer of 1626. Fifty years later an envoy whom the Sultan had sent to England, to complain that a Danish ship had kidnapped several natives, presented this garden to the Company in the name of his master (see a letter from the Court to the Sultan dated December 26, 1676); whereupon the envoy was nominated ‘keeper and overseer of the said ground’ on behalf of the Company.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-107" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-107" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Probably the Thomas Meriell who was appointed by the Company steward's mate on the <em>William</em> in January, 1635.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-108" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-108" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Can this have been Edward Winter?</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-109" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-109" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The charges, and Day's answers, are set forth in some detail, but they are sufficiently indicated in the previous correspondence.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-110" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-110" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">A very early example of this shortened form of 'China ware'.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-111" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-111" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Apparently a Brahman from Mangalagiri, in Guntūr District, where there is a celebrated pagoda.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-112" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-112" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">The head (<em>sar</em>) of the district (<em>samt</em>).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-113" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-113" aria-label="Return to text">3.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>silah-dār</em> ('bearing arms'). He was evidently one of the King's officers sent 'to put his fermand in excecution', as mentioned on p. 190.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-114" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-114" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Hind. <em>harkārā</em>, a messenger.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-115" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-115" aria-label="Return to text">2.</a> <span class="fn-body">Mahr. <em>durāhī</em>, Tel. <em>durāī</em>, a prohibition. 'They set a <em>De-Roy</em> on the Factory, which is a Prohibition in the King's Name for any one to have any thing to do with them till that be taken off' (Fryer's <em>New Account</em>, p. 31; see also p. 100).</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-116" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-116" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">Evidently the <em>Diamond</em> and the <em>Kit</em> (see p. 210)</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-117" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-117" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">These are obvious exaggerations.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-118" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-118" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">‘Imitate’ is meant.</span></li><li id="sec-06-fn-119" class="apparatus-item" role="doc-endnote"><a class="fn-backlink" href="#ref-sec-06-fn-119" aria-label="Return to text">1.</a> <span class="fn-body">The following is the account given of this incident by the Committee which investigated the dispute upon Hudson and Clark being sent home :—'Mr. Clarke being visitted not onely with boddily sicknes and infirmities but likewise quite deprived of all understanding, reason, and memory, insoemuch that hee was incapable of any the least employment, although then Agent : whereupon Hudson (as the said Clarke now affirmeth) came forcibly into the chamber of the said Clarke and caused him to bee putt into irons for the space of many daies; in which interim the said Hudson not onely seized upon the warehouses and whatsoever goods were therein, without taking inventory or any person to his assistance, but alsoe broke open all the chests, trunckes, and screetores which then were in his chamber and thereout did not onely take all his bookes of accompt, bills, speciallties, and whatsoever other papers belonged unto the said Company or him the said Clarke, but also at that tyme did take away the summe of 1,800 ryalls of eight ... being part of a greater summe formerly received of Captaine Weddall' (<em>Court Minutes</em>, January 21, 1642). The matter dragged on till May, 1642, when Hudson was acquitted of the charge relating to the 1,800 rials, for which Clark was ordered to give satisfaction. The same report charges Hudson with having given 1,047 pagodas 'to the Governour in money and goods of the Companies, pretending 25 dayes unjust imprisonment by the said Governour, and that this summe was exacted from him for his release'.
